A Sequent Calculus for General Inductive Definitions Note: This version is a draft from April 2026 and will be revised.
Robbe Van den Eede, Marc Denecker

Abstract
. Background: Inductive definitions are an important form of knowledge. The logic FO(ID) \xspace is an extension of classical first-order logic FO \xspace with a language construct to express general non-monotone inductive definitions, which was introduced as the outcome of a study of such definitions. Most existing proof systems for inductive definitions impose syntactic constraints on their definitions, thereby excluding many useful and natural definitions. Objectives: We aim to obtain a sequent calculus for FO(ID) \xspace that supports general non-monotone definitions. Methods: We extend an existing sequent calculus LKID \xspace by Brotherston and Simpson, founded on the principle of mathematical induction, to a sequent calculus SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace for FO(ID) \xspace . The main challenge in this extension is the accommodation of non-monotone inductive definitions. To overcome this challenge, we draw inspiration from the stable semantics, which is a commonly used semantics in logic programming that is closely related to the well-founded semantics behind FO(ID) \xspace . We corroborate SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace by establishing several proof-theoretical properties and through demonstration on various examples. Results: SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace covers the non-monotone definitions of FO(ID) \xspace . It is sound, both w.r.t. the well-founded and the stable semantics. Therefore, SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace is also suitable to prove theorems about (fragments of) logic programs under the well-founded and the stable semantics. Due to Gödel’s first incompleteness theorem, no logic capable of capturing the natural numbers is complete. Thus, like LKID \xspace , SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace cannot be complete, neither w.r.t. the well-founded nor the stable semantics. However, SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace is complete for a propositional fragment of FO(ID) \xspace w.r.t. the stable semantics, and for a first-order fragment of FO(ID) \xspace w.r.t. a weaker Henkin semantics. These results provide guarantees on the strength of SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace within the scope of what is theoretically feasible. Cut-elimination does not hold in general for SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace , but it holds for a fragment of FO(ID) \xspace consisting of positive definitions. This means that theorems in the given fragment can be proven without lemmas. Nevertheless, we prove a weakened version of cut-elimination for a fragment of FO(ID) \xspace consisting of stratified definitions. Furthermore, we establish a correspondence between SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace -proofs and SC FO \xspace \xspace -proofs from theories in which each definition is replaced by a natural FO \xspace induction scheme. We illustrate the versatility of SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace by proving various statements about non-monotone definitions. Incidentally, SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace allows for proofs of non-totality of definitions, offering a proof-theoretical analysis of paradoxes in ill-formed non-monotone definitions. Conclusions: SC FO(ID) \xspace \xspace is a theoretically substantiated sequent calculus for FO(ID) \xspace , enabling formal proofs of theorems involving general inductive definitions.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
General Inductive Definitions

Inductive definitions are an important form of knowledge, as they specify a range of useful notions in mathematics and computer science.
A prototypical example is the definition of the natural numbers via the following rules:

•

0
is a natural number.

•

If
n
is a natural number, then so is its successor
s(n)
.

An inductive definition defines a set by specifying how to construct it.
The
construction process
, often called the induction process, is naturally conceived as a process of iterated rule application, starting from the empty set, and terminating when the set is saturated under application of the rules.

The natural number definition is a
monotone
definition, meaning that the addition of objects to the defined set depends only on the presence of other objects, not on their absence.
Some definitions are non-monotone.
The
satisfaction relation

\models
in propositional logic is a binary relation between structures
\mathcal{I}
(seen as sets of propositional symbols) and propositional formulas
\varphi
(written in terms of
\land
and
\lnot
).
It is defined by the following rules:

•

\mathcal{I}\models p
if
p\in\mathcal{I}
.

•

\mathcal{I}\models\varphi\land\psi
if
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
and
\mathcal{I}\models\psi
.

•

\mathcal{I}\models\neg\varphi
if not
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
.

The third rule is non-monotone, since it derives pairs
(\mathcal{I},\lnot\varphi)
for the defined relation from the absence of pairs
(\mathcal{I},\varphi)
in it.
Non-monotone rules complicate the process of iterated rule application.
For example, every instance of the third rule is trivially applicable at the start of the construction process, when the set under construction is still empty and hence, “not
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
” is trivially satisfied.
Applying the rule at this point would be
unsafe
, however, as the construction process may later derive
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
.
As argued in
(
Denecker and Vennekens 2014
;
Bogaerts et al. 2018
)
, application of a non-monotone rule is to be delayed until the truth of its condition is
safely
established and cannot be falsified anymore.
The role of the well-founded induction order associated to this type of definition (here the subformula order on formulas
\varphi
) is to ensure safety; applying rules along this order, i.e., deriving smaller elements before larger ones, ensures that each rule application is safe.

FO(ID)
\xspace
is an extension of classical first-order logic with a language construct to represent a wide range of inductive definitions
(
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
)
.
More concretely, definitions are represented in FO(ID)
\xspace
as sets of
definitional rules
, which are expressions of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
Here
P(\bar{t})
is an atom called the
head
of the rule, and
\varphi
a first-order formula called the
body
of the rule.
For instance, the natural number definition from above can be formally represented in FO(ID)
\xspace
as

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Nat}(\mathit{zero})\leftarrow\top\\
\forall n:\mathit{Nat}(\mathit{succ}(n))\leftarrow\mathit{Nat}(n)\end{array}\right\}.

The logic FO(ID)
\xspace
has a somewhat convoluted history, with roots outside mathematical logic.
Its origins lie in the semantic troubles surrounding the logic programming language Prolog in the 1970s.
Prolog programs consist of
general program clauses
.
These are expressions of the form
Head

\colon
​​-
Body
, in which
Head
consists of an atom, and
Body
of any (finite) number of literals.
For some time, the logic programming community struggled to provide a coherent logical declarative interpretation for Prolog programs.
Denecker et al. proposed interpreting Prolog programs as non-monotone inductive definitions
(
Denecker et al. 2001
)
.
Denecker and Vennekens showed that the
well-founded semantics
, a formal semantics developed for logic programs with negation in the body
(
Van Gelder et al. 1991
)
, correctly computes the defined relation of non-monotone inductive definitions via three-valued evaluation, without requiring an induction order to ensure the safe application of rules
(
Denecker and Vennekens 2014
;
Denecker 1998
)
.
Later, Denecker and Ternovska proposed extending classical first-order logic with a language construct to express non-monotone inductive definitions

(
Denecker 2000
;
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
)
.
This resulted in the logic FO(ID)
\xspace
, which serves as an expressive knowledge representation language
1
1

1

In the field of Knowledge Representation, first-order logic is commonly used as a modeling language.
However, first-order logic has a rather limited expressivity, as it cannot represent certain elementary concepts such the set of natural numbers and the transitive closure of a graph.
Adding inductive definitions to first-order logic helps overcome these representational weaknesses.

and a formal scientific study of general inductive definitions.

Other logics of inductive definitions impose syntactic constraints on their definitions such as
positivity

(
Post 1943
;
Spector 1961
;
Moschovakis 1974
;
Aczel 1977
)
and
stratification

(
Kreisel 1963
;
Feferman 1970
;
Martin-Löf 1971
;
Buchholz et al. 1981
)
.
Positivity forbids negation in bodies of definitional rules, thus only admitting monotone definitions.
Stratification does permit negation in rule bodies, but only such that the definition can be decomposed into a hierarchy of positive definitions, each depending positively or negatively on concepts defined strictly lower in the hierarchy.
Although less restrictive than positivity, stratification still excludes many natural and useful definitions.
By not imposing these constraints, FO(ID)
\xspace
offers a general account of inductive definitions.
In particular, FO(ID)
\xspace
covers monotone definitions, definitions over a well-founded order, and
iterated inductive definitions

(
Kreisel 1963
;
Feferman 1970
;
Martin-Löf 1971
;
Buchholz et al. 1981
)
.

By discarding the stratification restriction, FO(ID)
\xspace
admits
non-total
definitions, for which the construction process terminates in a state where at least one rule is applicable, but no rule is safely applicable.
A simple example is
\{P\leftarrow\neg P\}
, which defines the propositional symbol
P
as its own negation.
In the initial state, where
P
is false, the single rule of this definition is applicable but not safely, since its application falsifies the condition of the rule.
The well-founded model assigns
undefined
to all atoms that are derivable but not safely derivable.

Non-total definitions are related to self-referential paradoxes.
For instance,
\{P\leftarrow\neg P\}
can be seen as a formalization of the
liar paradox
: “this sentence is false”, where
P
denotes the truth value of the sentence.
Alternatively, consider a version of the
barber paradox
in which a man named Jim is defined to shave himself, and a barber named Bob is defined to shave all men who do not shave themselves.
This can be formalized with the following definition in FO(ID)
\xspace
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Shaves}(\mathit{Jim},\mathit{Jim})\leftarrow\top\\
\forall m:\mathit{Shaves}(\mathit{Bob},m)\leftarrow\lnot\mathit{Shaves}(m,m)\end{array}\right\}

This definition is non-total, as it contains the paradoxical instance
\mathit{Shaves}(\mathit{Bob},\mathit{Bob})\leftarrow\lnot\mathit{Shaves}(\mathit{Bob},\mathit{Bob})
.
Paradoxical “definitions” are anomalies, to be avoided in practice.
But they are interesting anomalies, especially from the perspective of FO(ID)
\xspace
as formal scientific study of inductive definitions.
Paradoxical definitions can be stated in natural language, and the logic FO(ID)
\xspace
allows us to study such definitions, and to distinguish them from sensible, i.e., total definitions.
Nevertheless, when desired, one can always impose stratification on definitions in FO(ID)
\xspace
to rule out non-total definitions, or other more general conditions as investigated in
(
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
)
.

1.2.
A Sequent Calculus for General Inductive Definitions

Deductive inference is an important and useful form of reasoning that occupies a central position in logic.
In this paper, we investigate deductive inference for general inductive definitions by introducing a sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
for the logic FO(ID)
\xspace
.
A
sequent calculus
is a style of proof system due to Gentzen
(
Gentzen 1935
)
, which is well-known for its theoretical elegance and its goal-directed approach to theorem proving.
A proof system for FO(ID)
\xspace
enables formally proving theorems about general inductive definitions.
Proof systems have applications in
formal verification
, in which crucial properties of systems are logically derived from their implementation using proof assistants.
A more recent application of proof systems is in
proof logging

(
Heule 2021
;
Barbosa et al. 2023
;
Bogaerts et al. 2023
)
, in which combinatorial search algorithms are made
certifying

(
McConnell et al. 2011
)
by generating, alongside the output to a problem instance, a proof of its correctness.
This proof is then independently verified by a proof checker.

Our sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is based on the
principle of mathematical induction
, which is a technique to prove theorems about inductively defined sets using induction hypotheses.
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
extends the sequent calculus LKID
\xspace
by Brotherston and Simpson
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
, which formalizes the principle of mathematical induction for positive (hence, monotone) definitions.
The main challenge in this extension lies in handling the non-monotonicity of definitions in FO(ID)
\xspace
.
Our solution is surprisingly simple: in the induction rule, we replace only the positive occurrences of defined predicates by an induction hypothesis.
This solution has a theoretical basis in the
stable (model) semantics
from logic programming
(
Gelfond and Lifschitz 1988
)
.
While the stable semantics is no longer about inductive definitions, it can be seen as a good approximation of the well-founded semantics, which coincides with the well-founded semantics on every total (hence, on every ‘sensible’) definition.

1.3.
Related Work

Multiple proof systems have been developed for inductive definitions.
Martin-Löf introduced an intuitionistic natural deduction system (another style of proof system due to Gentzen
(
Gentzen 1935
)
) for iterated inductive definitions
(
Martin-Löf 1971
)
.
McDowell and Miller
(
McDowell and Miller 2000
)
as well as Momigliano and Tiu
(
Momigliano and Tiu 2004
)
provided sequent calculus counterparts to this system.
Brotherston and Simpson developed classical sequent calculi for a more restricted class of inductive definitions, formalizing the principles of mathematical induction and infinite descent
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.
Finally, Hou Ping et al. devised a classical sequent calculus for the propositional fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
(
Hou et al. 2007
)
, and Hou Ping and Denecker devised a sequent calculus for an extension of FO(ID)
\xspace
with least fixpoint constructions
(
Hou and Denecker 2009
)
.

The sequent calculi by Brotherston and Simpson are restricted to positive definitions, and the proof systems by Martin-Löf, McDowell and Miller, and Momigliano and Tiu to stratified definitions.
The sequent calculus by Hou Ping et al.
(
Hou et al. 2007
)
supports non-stratified definitions, but only propositional ones.
The sequent calculus by Hou Ping and Denecker
(
Hou and Denecker 2009
)
, however, supports first-order non-stratified definitions.
Our sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
differs from their calculus, which we here call HD, in the following ways:

•

HD is based more directly on the well-founded semantics, as it uses the notion of
unfounded set

(
Van Gelder et al. 1991
)
.
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, on the other hand, is based on the principle of mathematical induction, extending the sequent calculus LKID
\xspace
by Brotherston and Simpson
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.

•

HD supports an extension of FO(ID)
\xspace
with
least fixpoint expressions

(
Compton 1994
)
.
Adding least fixpoint expressions to the language of FO(ID)
\xspace
enlarges the class of expressible sets.
However, one inference rule for these least fixpoint expressions is
infinitary
, meaning that it has infinitely many premises.
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
only has finitary inference rules,
but it does not support least fixpoint expressions.

•

Contrary to HD, SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is sound w.r.t. both the well-founded and the stable semantics.
Therefore, SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
can additionally be used to prove theorems about (fragments of) logic programs under either semantics.
2
2

2

SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
can not only capture entire logic programs, but also
fragments
of logic programs, leaving the remainder of the program open.
For example, given a logic program that contains facts about an edge relation
E/2
and a definition of the transitive closure
T/2
of
E/2
, SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
can capture the definition of
T/2
independently of the facts about
E/2
.

The fact that SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is sound w.r.t. the two semantics demarcates its deductive strength, as it can only prove statements that are valid under both semantics.
When used to reason about inductive definitions, this constraint is rather minor, since the two semantics correspond on all total definitions.

1.4.
Contributions

In previous work, we introduced the sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and proved its soundness w.r.t. the well-founded semantics
(
Van den Eede et al. 2024
)
.
This paper extends our previous work by adding:

•

a more detailed discussion on the intuitions behind SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and additional examples;

•

an explicit link between SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and the stable semantics;

•

several proof-theoretical results:

–

a correspondence between theorems in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and theorems in a sequent calculus SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
for classical first-order logic FO
\xspace
, in which definitions are replaced by an (infinite) approximating FO
\xspace
-schema;

–

soundness w.r.t. the stable semantics;

–

completeness w.r.t. the stable semantics for a propositional fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
and completeness w.r.t. the weaker
Henkin semantics
for a first-order fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
;

–

cut-elimination for a fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
consisting of positive definitions, and a weakened version of cut-elimination for a fragment consisting of stratified definitions.
As shown by multiple counterexamples, cut-elimination does not hold in general for FO(ID)
\xspace
.

1.5.
Overview

The paper is structured as follows.
In Section
2
, we introduce the syntax and the well-founded semantics for FO(ID)
\xspace
.
In Section
3
, we present the sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and prove a few propositions about the inference rules for definitions.
In Section
4
, we establish a correspondence between theorems of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and theorems of SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
.
In Section
5
, we define the stable semantics and the Henkin semantics for FO(ID)
\xspace
.
In Sections
6
,
7
and
8
, we prove the aforementioned theoretical results on soundness, completeness and cut-elimination, respectively.
Finally, we provide a summary and possible directions for future research in Section
9
.
Technical proofs of several lemmas and propositions are included in the appendix.

2.
FO(ID)
\xspace

Large parts of this section appear in earlier papers
(
Denecker and Vennekens 2014
;
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
;
Denecker et al. 2001
)
.

2.1.
Syntax

The syntax of FO(ID)
\xspace
extends the syntax of first-order logic (FO
\xspace
), which is defined as usual.
A
vocabulary
is a set of non-logical symbols, which are subdivided into predicate symbols and function symbols.
We write
\mathsf{ar}(\sigma)
to refer to the arity of a predicate or function symbol
\sigma
, and
\sigma/n
to indicate that
\sigma
has arity
n
.
A
propositional symbol
is a
0
-ary predicate symbol, and a
object symbol
is a
0
-ary function symbol.

Terms
are built from function symbols.

Atoms
are expressions of the form
P(\bar{t})
,
t=s
,
\top
or
\bot
, where
P/n
is a predicate symbol,
\bar{t}
an
n
-tuple of terms,
t
and
s
terms, and
\top
and
\bot
Boolean constants representing truth and falsity, respectively.
(FO
\xspace
-)
formulas
are built from atoms with propositional connectives
\lnot,\land,\lor,\Rightarrow,\Leftrightarrow
and quantifiers
\forall,\exists
.
If all non-logical symbols in a formula
\varphi
occur in a vocabulary
\Sigma
, we call
\varphi
a formula
over

\Sigma
.
We write
t\neq s
for
\lnot(t=s)
.
We write
\bar{t}
for a tuple of terms
(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
.
Given another tuple of terms
\bar{s}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n})
, we write
\bar{t}=\bar{s}
for
t_{1}=s_{1}\land\dots\land t_{n}=s_{n}
.
Given a tuple of object symbols
\bar{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, we write
\forall\bar{x}:\varphi
for
\forall x_{1}:\forall x_{2}:\dots\forall x_{n}:\varphi
and similarly for
\exists\bar{x}:\varphi
.
Given terms
t
,
s
and an object symbol
s
, we denote by
t[s/x]
the term obtained from
t
by replacing all occurrences of
x
in
t
by
s
.
An occurrence of a object symbol
x
in a formula
\varphi
is said to be
bound
if it is in the scope of a quantified subformula
\exists x:\psi
or
\forall x:\psi
of
\varphi
, and is said to be
free
otherwise.
Note that we do not explicitly distinguish between variables and constants.
Such distinction can be made implicitly by seeing free occurrences of objects symbols in a formula as constants and bound occurrences as variables.
The set of freely occurring object symbols in a formula
\varphi
is denoted by
\free(\varphi)
.
For a set of formulas
\Gamma
, we write
\free(\Gamma)
for
\cup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\free(\varphi)
.
Given a term
t
, an object symbol
x
and a formula
\varphi
, we denote by
\varphi[t/x]
the formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing all free occurrences of
x
in
\varphi
by
t
.
The substitution is safe if
t
contains no object symbol
y
that gets bound in
\varphi[t/x]
due to the existence of a free occurrence of
x
in the scope of a quantification
\forall y
or
\exists y
within
\varphi
.
We will silently assume that such quantifications in
\varphi
are renamed prior to the substitution, so that substitution is always safe.
Given a set of formulas
\Gamma
, we write
\Gamma[t/x]
for the set
\cup_{\varphi\in\Gamma}\varphi[t/x]
.
An occurrence of a subformula
\psi
in a formula
\varphi
is said to be
positive
if it occurs in the scope of an even number of negation symbols
\lnot
(after unwinding all biconditionals
\Leftrightarrow
and material implications
\Rightarrow
in terms of
\land
,
\lor
and
\lnot
), and is said to be
negative
otherwise.
We use the symbol
\doteq
for syntactic equality.

Definition 2.1.

A
definitional rule
(or
rule
for short) is an expression of the form

\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi

where
\bar{x}
is a tuple of object symbols,
P
a predicate symbol,
\bar{t}
an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of terms, and
\varphi
an FO
\xspace
-formula.
The atom
P(\bar{t})
is called the
head
and the formula
\varphi
the
body
of the definitional rule.
An object symbol
y
is said to
occur freely
in a definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
if it occurs freely in the head
P(\bar{t})
or the body
\varphi
of the rule, and is not among
\bar{x}
.
An (FO(ID)
\xspace
-)
definition
is a finite set
\Phi
of definitional rules such that for any definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
in
\Phi
, no object symbol in
\bar{x}
occurs freely in any (other) definitional rule in
\Phi
.
A predicate symbol
P
that occurs in the head of a definitional rule of a definition
\Phi
is said to be a
defined predicate
of
\Phi
.
A
defined atom
is an atom
P(\bar{t})
where
P
is a defined predicate of
\Phi
.
All other non-logical symbols occurring in
\Phi
are called
parameters
of
\Phi
.
The set of defined predicates of
\Phi
is denoted by
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, the set of parameters of
\Phi
by
\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)
, and their union by
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
.
We say that
\Phi
defines its defined predicates in terms of its parameters.
An object symbol
y
is said to
occur freely
in
\Phi
if it occurs freely in a definitional rule of
\Phi
.
We denote the set of all freely occurring object symbols in
\Phi
by
\free(\Phi)
.

Inductive definitions define a concept by specifying how to construct its values from the values of its parameter concepts.
Intuitively, the construction proceeds by iteratively applying the definitional rules.
3
3

3

Martin-Löf refers to definitional rules as
productions
, emphasizing the constructive nature of inductive definitions.

Concretely, an instance of a definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
becomes applicable during the construction process when its body is derived to (safely) hold in the relation being constructed.
Applying the rule amounts to extending the value of
P
with the instance of
t
.
The connective
\leftarrow
is referred to as the
definitional implication
.
We will see that the presence of a definitional rule in a definition logically entails the corresponding material implication.
Yet, the two connectives
\leftarrow
and
\Rightarrow
should not be confused.
An important difference is that definitional rules are not
truth-conditional
, meaning that they are not assigned truth values in structures.
Definitions, on the other hand,
are
truth-conditional in FO(ID)
\xspace
, as will be clarified in Section
2.2
.
Informally, a definition
\Phi
is satisfied in a structure
\mathcal{I}
if
\mathcal{I}
interprets the defined predicates of
\Phi
as specified by the rules of
\Phi
.

FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas
are built from atoms and definitions with propositional connectives and quantifiers.
Hence, definitions are an explicit part of the syntax of FO(ID)
\xspace
, and they can occur in complex formulas.

Example 2.2.

Let there be a universe of nodes, let
G
be a graph on them and let
s
be a particular node.
A node
t
is said to be
reachable
(from
s
, through
G
) if there exists a path from
s
to
t
along the edges of
G
.
We can define the set of reachable nodes from
s
with an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition of a predicate
\mathit{Reachable}/1
, in terms of a predicate
\mathit{Edge}/2
, representing the edges of
G
, and a constant
s
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Reachable}(s)\leftarrow\top\\
\forall x,y:\mathit{Reachable}(y)\leftarrow\mathit{Reachable}(x)\land\mathit{Edge}(x,y)\end{array}\right\}

Denote this definition by
\Phi
.
The FO(ID)
\xspace
-formula
\exists s:\Phi\land\forall t:\mathit{Reachable}(t)
expresses that
G
has a node with a path to every other node in
G
, and
\forall s:\Phi\land\forall t:\mathit{Reachable}(t)
expresses that every node in
G
has a path to every other node in
G
, i.e., that
G
is
strongly connected
.

Below is an example of a definition by
simultaneous
or
mutual induction
.

Example 2.3.

The sets of even and odd numbers can be mutually defined by an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition of a predicate
\mathit{Even}/2
and a predicate
\mathit{Odd}/2
, in terms of the successor function
\mathit{succ}/1
and the constant
\mathit{zero}
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Even}(\mathit{zero})\leftarrow\top\\
\forall n:\mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(n))\leftarrow\mathit{Odd}(n)\\
\forall n:\mathit{Odd}(\mathit{succ}(n))\leftarrow\mathit{Even}(n)\end{array}\right\}

Several logics
(
Kreisel 1963
;
Feferman 1970
;
Martin-Löf 1971
;
Buchholz et al. 1981
;
McDowell and Miller 2000
;
Momigliano and Tiu 2004
)
support
stratified
inductive definitions, which are more general than monotone definitions.

Definition 2.4.

Let
\Phi
be a definition.
A
stratification
of
\Phi
is a function
\ell:\mathrm{def}(\Phi)\to\mathbb{N}
such that for every definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
of
\Phi
:

(i)

\ell(Q)\leq\ell(P)
for every
Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
that occurs in
\varphi
;

(ii)

\ell(Q)<\ell(P)
for every
Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
that occurs negatively in
\varphi
.

If a stratification of
\Phi
exists, we say that
\Phi
is
stratified
.

A stratification
\ell
of a definition
\Phi
induces a hierarchy of monotone subdefinitions.

A stratified definition can be seen as one that is splittable in a hierarchy of monotone subdefinitions.

Example 2.5.

The following definition is stratified:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Reachable}(s)\leftarrow\top\\
\forall x,y:\mathit{Reachable}(y)\leftarrow\mathit{Reachable}(x)\land\mathit{Edge}(x,y)\\
\forall x:\mathit{Unreachable}(x)\leftarrow\lnot\mathit{Reachable}(x)\end{array}\right\}

Indeed,
\ell(\mathit{Reachable})=0
and
\ell(\mathit{Unreachable})=1
specify a stratification for this definition.

A stratified definition can be seen as one that can be split up in a hierarchy of monotone subdefinitions.
For the definition in the previous example, for instance, this hierarchy consists of the monotone subdefinitions

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Reachable}(s)\leftarrow\top\\
\forall x,y:\mathit{Reachable}(y)\leftarrow\mathit{Reachable}(x)\land\mathit{Edge}(x,y)\end{array}\right\}

and

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\forall x:\mathit{Unreachable}(x)\leftarrow\lnot\mathit{Reachable}(x)\end{array}\right\}.

Even though stratified definitions are more general than monotone definitions, there are many examples of inductive definitions that are not stratified:

Example 2.6.

While commonly defined via a monotone definition, the set of even natural numbers can also be defined via a non-monotone definition by structural induction.
In FO(ID)
\xspace
, it would be expressed as follows:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Even}(\mathit{zero})\leftarrow\top\\
\forall n:\mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(n))\leftarrow\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(n)\end{array}\right\}

This definition defines the predicate
\mathit{Even}/1
in terms of the constant
\mathit{zero}
, the function
\mathit{succ}/1
and the natural number predicate
\mathit{Nat}/1
.
There exists no stratification
\ell
of this definition, as the second definitional rule would impose
\ell(\mathit{Even})<\ell(\mathit{Even})
.

Example 2.7.

The satisfaction relation
\models
for propositional logic can be defined by an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition of a predicate
\mathit{Sat}/2
, in terms of a predicate
\mathit{Member}/2
and constructor functions
\mathit{and}/2
and
\mathit{not}/1
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\forall i,p:\mathit{Sat}(i,p)\leftarrow\mathit{Member}(p,i)\\
\forall i,f,g:\mathit{Sat}(i,and(f,g))\leftarrow\mathit{Sat}(i,f)\land\mathit{Sat}(i,g)\\
\forall i,f:\mathit{Sat}(i,not(f))\leftarrow\lnot\mathit{Sat}(i,f)\end{array}\right\}

The variable
i
represents a structure,
p
a propositional symbol, and
f
and
g
propositional formulas.
The term
and(f,g)
represents the conjunction of
f
and
g
, and
not(f)
the negation of
f
.
The formula
\mathit{Member}(p,i)
expresses that
p
is an element of
i
, when viewing
i
as a set of propositional symbols.
By a similar reasoning as in Example
2.6
, this definition is not stratified.

Example 2.8.

The distance from a node
n
to a node
m
in a graph is the length of the shortest path from
n
to
m
if such path exists, and is undefined otherwise.
This notion can be defined by an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition of a predicate
\mathit{Distance}/3
, containing all triples
(x,y,n)
for which
x
and
y
are nodes in the graph with distance
n
from
x
to
y
, in terms of the constant
\mathit{zero}
, function
\mathit{succ}/1
, predicate
\mathit{Edge}/2
, and order relation
\leq\!\!/2
(used with infix notation):

\left\{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{l}\forall x:\mathit{Distance}(x,x,\mathit{zero})\leftarrow\top\\
\forall x,y,n:\mathit{Distance}(x,y,succ(n))\leftarrow(\exists z:\mathit{Distance}(x,z,n)\land\mathit{Edge}(z,y))\land\lnot(\exists m:m\leq n\land\mathit{Distance}(x,y,m))\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt\right\}

Since the defined predicate
\mathit{Distance}
occurs negatively in the body of the second rule, this definition is neither monotone nor stratified.

Example 2.9.

Consider a graph in which edges can be activated at each time point.
Once activated, an edge remains active until a path of active edges emerges from a given node
a
to a given node
b
.
If this happens, all edges become inactive again.
The state of the active edges can be modeled by an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition of a predicate
\mathit{Active}/3
, together with an auxiliary predicate
\mathit{ActivePath}/3
, in terms of the function
\mathit{succ}/1
and predicate
\mathit{Activate}/3
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\forall n,m,t:\mathit{Active}(n,m,\mathit{succ}(t))\leftarrow\mathit{Activate}(n,m,t)\land\lnot\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)\\
\forall n,m,t:\mathit{Active}(n,m,\mathit{succ}(t))\leftarrow\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\land\lnot\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)\\
\forall n,m,t:\mathit{ActivePath}(n,m,t)\leftarrow\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\\
\forall n,m,p,t:\mathit{ActivePath}(n,p,t)\leftarrow\mathit{ActivePath}(n,m,t)\land\mathit{ActivePath}(m,p,t)\end{array}\right\}

The atom
\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)
says that the edge from
n
to
m
is active at time point
t
,
\mathit{Activate}(n,m,t)
says that this edge is activated at time point
t
, and
\mathit{ActivePath}(n,m,t)
says that there is a path of active edges from
n
to
m
at time point
t
.
Time is modeled by the natural numbers.
The last two rules define the transitive closure of all active edges at a given time point.
This definition does not admit a stratification
\ell
, as the third and the first rule would impose
\ell(\mathit{Active})\leq\ell(\mathit{ActivePath})<\ell(\mathit{Active})
.

Through variations, this abstract example can be converted into real-world examples, such as the creation of electric circuits that burn through once a short circuit has been formed, or the construction of towers that collapse after a threshold height/weight has been exceeded.

Example 2.10.

Consider a system with a multiple users and a file.
A user has access to the file if they are the owner, or if they are granted access by a user with access while not blocked by a user with access.
The delegation of access can be modeled by an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition of a predicate
\mathit{Access}/1
in terms of a constant
\mathit{owner}
and predicates
\mathit{Grants}/2
and
\mathit{Blocks}/2
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Access}(\mathit{owner})\leftarrow\top\\
\forall u:\mathit{Access}(u)\leftarrow(\exists v:\mathit{Access}(v)\land\mathit{Grants}(v,u))\land\lnot(\exists v:\mathit{Access}(v)\land\mathit{Blocks}(v,u))\end{array}\right\}

This definition is not stratified, as the defined predicate
\mathit{Access}
occurs negatively in the body of the second rule.

In general, a definition
\Phi
contains multiple rules
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
per predicate
P
.
These rules can be merged into a single rule
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\leftarrow\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})
without altering the (informal) meaning of the definition.

Definition 2.11.

A definition
\Phi
is said to be
normal
if, for every defined predicate
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, there is precisely one definitional rule in
\Phi
with
P
in its head, which is of the form
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\leftarrow\varphi
.
The
normalization

\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
of a definition
\Phi
is the definition consisting of one rule
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\leftarrow\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})
per defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
, where
\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})
is the of all formulas
\exists\bar{x}:\bar{y}=\bar{t}\land\varphi
such that
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
is a rule in
\Phi
.
4
4

4

Since the tuples
\bar{y}
are not unique,
the
normalization of
\Phi
is technically ill-defined.
However, since the choice of
\bar{y}
is semantically insignificant, we still speak of
the
normalization by abuse of terminology.

Here
\bar{y}
does not include object symbols quantified in front of a rule in
\Phi
defining
P
.
We refer to
\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})
as the
merged body
of
P
in
\Phi
w.r.t.
\bar{y}
.
We will often write
\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
for
\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})
if
\Phi
is clear from the context.
Given an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of terms
\bar{t}
, we write
\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{t})
for
\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})[\bar{t}/\bar{y}]
, which we call the
merged body
of
P(\bar{t})
.

Example 2.12.

Let
\Phi
be the definition of even numbers from Example
2.6
.
The merged body
\varphi_{\mathit{Even,\Phi}}(y)
of
\mathit{Even}(y)
w.r.t.
\Phi
is
y=\mathit{zero}\lor\exists x:y=\mathit{succ}(x)\land Nat(x)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(x)
.
5
5

5

Technically speaking,
\varphi_{\mathit{Even,\Phi}}(y)
is equal to
(y=\mathit{zero}\land\top)\lor\exists x:y=\mathit{succ}(x)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(x)
. However, we sometimes remove
\top
for simplicity.

Hence, the normalization
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
of
\Phi
is

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\forall y:\mathit{Even}(y)\leftarrow y=\mathit{zero}\lor\exists x:y=\mathit{succ}(x)\land\mathit{Nat}(x)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(x)\end{array}\right\}.

2.2.
Well-founded Semantics

For monotone inductive definitions, the standard construction process starts from the assumption that all defined facts are false, and then gradually revises some of these assumptions through iterated rule application.
Once saturation is reached, i.e., once there are no more applicable rules, it concludes that all defined atoms that have not been derived to be true must be false.
At each point of the construction process, partial information about the defined atoms is available: atoms that are true at an intermediate stage have reached their defined value, but atoms that are false at an intermediate stage not necessarily; these may still be derived true at a later stage.

The construction process for monotone definitions does not work for non-monotone definitions, since non-monotone rules can only be applied safely when it is certain that the negative subformulas
\neg\psi
in their bodies are true, i.e., that
\psi
will not be derived later in the construction process.
Based on the well-founded semantics for logic programs
(
Van Gelder et al. 1991
)
, Denecker and Vennekens introduced a new formalization of the construction process
(
Denecker and Vennekens 2014
)
.
By re-formalizing the construction process as a sequence of three-valued structures of increasing precision, it was made explicit at each stage of the process whether a defined fact was derived to be true (
\mathbf{t}
), derived to be false (
\mathbf{f}
), or not yet derived, i.e., still unknown (
\mathbf{u}
).
As in the monotone case, a fact is derived to be true if the body of one of its rules is true in the current three-valued structure.
Deriving an unknown fact to be (certainly) false is more subtle.
It uses the notion of
unfounded set

(
Van Gelder et al. 1991
)
, which is a set of unknown atoms such that, if all of these atoms are set to false, then the bodies of all the rules deriving these facts become false as well.
Intuitively, the atoms in an unfounded set can only make each other true, and therefore, they can be safely derived to be false.
We we will formalize the aforementioned notions in the remainder of this section.

(Two-valued) structures
\mathcal{I}
are defined as usual, consisting of a set
D
called the
domain
of
\mathcal{I}
, also denoted by
\mathsf{dom}(\Phi)
, and a mapping from non-logical symbols
\sigma
to appropriate values
\sigma^{\mathcal{I}}
in
D
, called the
interpretation
of
\sigma
in
\mathcal{I}
.
In particular,
\sigma^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq D^{n}
if
\sigma
is an
n
-ary predicate symbol, and
\sigma^{\mathcal{I}}:D^{n}\to D
if
\sigma
is an
n
-ary function symbol.
By abuse of notation, we will view the interpretation of a propositional symbol as a Boolean constant,
\mathbf{f}
or
\mathbf{t}
(standing for ‘false’ and ‘true’, respectively),
and the interpretation of an object symbol as an element of
D
.
The set of interpreted symbols of
\mathcal{I}
is called the
vocabulary

\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
of
\mathcal{I}
.
If
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\Sigma
, we cal
\mathcal{I}
a
\Sigma
-structure
.
We say that a structure
\mathcal{I}

interprets
an expression
\epsilon
(a term or a formula) if all freely occurring non-logical symbols in
\epsilon
are interpreted in
\mathcal{I}
.
In this case, we call
\epsilon
an expression
over

\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
.
Given a tuple of terms
\bar{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})
interpreted by a structure
\mathcal{I}
, we write
\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}}
for
(t_{1}^{\mathcal{I}},\dots,t_{n}^{\mathcal{I}})
.

We say that a structure
\mathcal{I}

expands
a structure
\mathcal{J}
if
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{J})\subseteq\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
,
\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})=\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{J})
and
\sigma^{\mathcal{I}}
=
\sigma^{\mathcal{J}}
for all
\sigma\in\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{J})
.
Given a structure
\mathcal{I}
with domain
D
, a tuple of non-logical symbols
\bar{\sigma}
, and a tuple of corresponding values
\bar{\alpha}
in
D
, we denote by
\mathcal{I}[\bar{\sigma}:\bar{\alpha}]
the structure obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by interpreting
\bar{\sigma}
by
\bar{\alpha}
.
If
\Sigma
is a subset of the vocabulary of
\mathcal{I}
, then
\mathcal{I}|_{\Sigma}
denotes the structure obtained from
\mathcal{I}
by restricting its interpretation mapping to
\Sigma
.

Denecker and Vennekens formalize the construction processes behind inductive definitions as
well-founded inductions
, which are (possibly transfinite) sequences of increasingly precise
three-valued structures

(
Denecker and Vennekens 2014
)
.
These structures have three different
truth values
:
\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{t}
, where
\mathbf{u}
stands for ‘unknown’.
We consider two orders on the set of truth values: the
truth order

\leq_{\mathrm{t}}
and the
precision order

\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
,
defined by
\mathbf{f}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathbf{u}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathbf{t}
, and
\mathbf{u}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathbf{f}
,
\mathbf{u}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathbf{t}
respectively.
We also write
\lnot\mathbf{f}\coloneq\mathbf{t}
,
\lnot\mathbf{u}\coloneq\mathbf{u}
and
\lnot\mathbf{t}\coloneq\mathbf{f}
.
A three-valued structure
\mathfrak{A}
is defined similarly as a two-valued structure, except that
n
-ary predicate symbols are interpreted as functions from
D^{n}
to
\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}\}
, where
D
is the domain of
\mathfrak{A}
.
We extend the truth and precision order pointwisely to functions with codomain
\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}\}
, i.e., given
f,g:X\to\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{u},\mathbf{t}\}
, we let
f\leq_{\mathrm{t}}g
iff
f(x)\leq_{\mathrm{t}}g(x)
for all
x\in X
, and similarly for
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
.
Furthermore, we extend these orders to three-valued structures
\mathfrak{A},\mathfrak{B}
, by letting
\mathfrak{A}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{B}
iff
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
have the same domain and vocabulary, the same interpretation for non-predicate symbols, and
P^{\mathfrak{A}}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}P^{\mathfrak{B}}
for every predicate symbol
P
, and similarly for
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
.
Note that a two-valued structure can be seen as a special case of a three-valued structure, by identifying a subset
S
of
D^{n}
with its characteristic function, sending every element of
S
to
\mathbf{t}
and every non-element to
\mathbf{f}
.
In this way, two-valued structures correspond to maximally precise three-valued structures.
In the sequel, we will frequently abuse this correspondence, for instance by writing
P^{\mathcal{I}}(\bar{a})=\mathbf{t}
instead of
\bar{a}\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
for two-valued structures
\mathcal{I}
.

Given an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
and a three-valued structure
\mathfrak{A}
, we define the
truth value
or
truth assignment

\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}
of
\varphi
in
\mathfrak{A}
by structural induction on
\varphi
, via the following rules:
6
6

6

The defined truth assignment is known as Kleene’s truth assignment.
In
(
Denecker and Vennekens 2014
)
, Denecker and Vennekens also allow other truth assignments.
The only conditions that such an assignment must satisfy are
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
-monotonicity (i.e.,
\mathfrak{A}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
implies
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\varphi^{\mathfrak{B}}
) and restriction to the standard truth assignment on two-valued structures.
Another sensible truth assignment, for instance, is
supervaluation
, which defines
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}
as
\mathsf{glb}_{\leq_{\mathrm{p}}}\{\varphi^{\mathcal{I}}\mid\mathcal{I}\text{ is two-valued and }\mathfrak{A}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathcal{I}\}

(
van Fraassen 1966
)
.
Supervaluation is more precise than Kleene’s truth assignment.
For instance, for
P^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{u}
, Kleene’s truth assignment of
P\lor\lnot P
is
\mathbf{u}
, while the supervaluation of
P\lor\lnot P
is
\mathbf{t}
.
Different truth assignments lead to different notions of
well-founded model
.
Kripke’s truth assignments leads to the
standard
well-founded model, while supervaluation leads to the
ultimate
well-founded model
(
Denecker et al. 2004
)
.
We restrict to Kleene’s truth assignment for simplicity, but since our proof of soundness w.r.t. the well-founded semantics does not rely on the specifics of Kleene’s truth assignment, SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is also sound w.r.t. other notions of the well-founded semantics corresponding to different truth assignments.

•

(t=s)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
if
t^{\mathfrak{A}}=s^{\mathfrak{A}}
and
(t=s)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
if
t^{\mathfrak{A}}\neq s^{\mathfrak{A}}
;

•

P(\bar{t})^{\mathfrak{A}}=P^{\mathcal{I}}\!(\bar{t}^{\mathfrak{A}})
;

•

(\lnot\varphi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\lnot\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}
;

•

(\varphi\land\psi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}},\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}\}
;

•

(\forall x:\varphi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}[x:a]}\mid a\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathfrak{A})\}
.

The rules for
\lor
,
\Rightarrow
,
\Leftrightarrow
and
\exists
can de derived from the above rules through reformulation in terms of
\lnot
,
\land
and
\forall
.
The value
t^{\mathfrak{A}}
of a term
t
in a three-valued structure
\mathfrak{A}
is defined as for two-valued structures, via the rule
f(t_{1},\dots,t_{n})^{\mathfrak{A}}=f^{\mathfrak{A}}(t_{1}^{\mathfrak{A}},\dots,t_{n}^{\mathfrak{A}})
.
We extend the satisfaction relation
\models
for FO
\xspace
to three-valued structures by letting
\mathfrak{A}\models\varphi
iff
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
.
It is straightforward to verify that this truth assignment is
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
-monotone, i.e.,
\mathfrak{A}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
implies
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\varphi^{\mathfrak{B}}
;
and that if
\mathfrak{A}
is two-valued, then
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}
takes the standard truth value of
\varphi
in
\mathfrak{A}
.

Given a definition
\Phi
, the interpretation of the defined predicates of
\Phi
generally depends on the interpretation of the parameters of
\Phi
.
A structure with vocabulary
\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)
is called a
\Phi
-
context
.
For the remainder of the section, we fix a definition
\Phi
and a
\Phi
-context
\mathcal{O}
, and come to the notion of
well-founded model
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
.

Given a tuple of non-logical symbols
\bar{\sigma}
(possibly) occurring in a formula
\varphi
, together with a tuple of corresponding values
\bar{\alpha}
in
D
, we write
\varphi(\bar{\alpha}/\bar{\sigma})^{\mathfrak{A}}
, or shortly
\varphi(\bar{\alpha})^{\mathfrak{A}}
, to refer to
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}[\bar{\sigma}:\bar{\alpha}]}
.
A
domain atom
is a pair
(P,\bar{a})
, where
P
is a predicate symbol and
\bar{a}\in D^{\mathsf{ar}(P)}
.
With an abuse of notation, we will often write
P(\bar{a})
instead of
(P,\bar{a})
.
Furthermore, we write
P(\bar{a})^{\mathfrak{A}}
for
P^{\mathfrak{A}}\!(\bar{a})
, and
\varphi_{P(\bar{a})}^{\mathfrak{A}}
for
\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})^{\mathfrak{A}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]}
, where
\bar{y}
is an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols not occurring freely in the body
\varphi
of any definitional rule of
\Phi
of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
Given a set of predicate symbols
\Pi
, we denote by
At_{D}^{\Pi}
the set of domain atoms
(P,\bar{a})
for which
P\in\Pi
.

Definition 2.13.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a three-valued
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
-structure with domain
D
.
A three-valued
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
-structure
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
is said to be a
\Phi
-refinement
of
\mathfrak{A}
if there exists a non-empty set
U\subseteq At_{D}^{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}
such that
A^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{u}
for all
A\in U
, and either

•

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{A}[U:\mathbf{t}]
and for all
A\in U
,
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
, or

•

\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{A}[U:\mathbf{f}]
and for all
A\in U
,
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}}=\mathbf{f}
.

Here
\mathfrak{A}[U:\mathbf{t}]
refers to the structure identical to
\mathfrak{A}
, except that its interpretation of
P
maps
\bar{a}
to
\mathbf{t}
for all
(P,\bar{a})\in U
, and similarly for
\mathfrak{A}[U:\mathbf{f}]
.

Note the asymmetry between deriving truth and falsity of defined atoms: the truth of defined atoms can only be derived if the corresponding bodies are true in
\mathfrak{A}
, while the falsity of defined atoms can be derived if this implies the falsity of the corresponding bodies in
\mathfrak{A}^{\prime}
.
The second kind of refinement is based on the notion of
unfounded set
in the well-founded semantics for logic programs
(
Van Gelder et al. 1991
)
.

Definition 2.14.

A
well-founded induction
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
is a sequence
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
of three-valued
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
-structures extending
\mathcal{O}
, such that:

•

P^{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}(\bar{a})=\mathbf{u}
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
and all
\bar{a}\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathfrak{A}_{0})^{\mathsf{ar}(P)}
;

•

\mathfrak{A}_{i+1}
is a
\Phi
-refinement of
\mathfrak{A}_{i}
for every ordinal
0\leq i<\beta
; and

•

\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}
is the
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
-limit of
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{i<\lambda}
for every limit ordinal
0<\lambda\leq\beta
.

A well-founded induction
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
is called
terminal
if its
limit

\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}
has no
\Phi
-refinement.
The
well-founded model
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
is the limit of any terminal well-founded induction of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
.
If this well-founded model is two-valued, we say that
\Phi
is
total
in
\mathcal{O}
.
If
\Phi
is total in any
\Phi
-context, we say that
\Phi
is
total
.

Remark 2.15.

Note that well-founded inductions are
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
-increasing, which guarantees that the
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
-limits are well-defined.
Since the truth function is
\leq_{\mathrm{p}}
-monotone, well-founded inductions
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
have the property that once
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{t}\}
for some
i
, then
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}=\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}_{j}}
for all
j\geq i
.

The following result by Denecker and Vennekens
(
Denecker and Vennekens 2007
)
guarantees that the notion of well-founded model of a definition in a context is well-defined.

Theorem 2.16.

Any definition has a terminal well-founded induction in any context.
Furthermore, all terminal well-founded inductions of a given definition in a given context have the same limit.

Non-totality of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
means that the well-founded induction of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
leave at least one defined atom unknown.
This usually indicates a kind of flaw in the definition (or in the interpretation of the parameters).

Example 2.17.

Let
\Phi
be the even number definition from Example
2.6
, and let
\mathcal{O}
be the
\Phi
-context with
\mathsf{dom}(\Phi)=\mathbb{N}
, and with the standard interpretation for
\mathit{zero}
and
\mathit{succ}
.
Recall that
\varphi_{\mathit{Even}}(y)\doteq(y=\mathit{zero})\lor\exists x:y=s(x)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(x)
.
As can be checked, the assignments

\displaystyle E^{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}(n)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u}\,\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle E^{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t},\quad E^{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}(n)=\mathbf{u}\,\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}

\displaystyle E^{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t},\quad E^{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}(1)=\mathbf{f},\quad E^{\mathfrak{A}_{2}}(n)=\mathbf{u}\,\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0,1\}

\displaystyle\;\;\vdots

\displaystyle E^{\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}}(n)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t}\,\text{ for }n\in 2\mathbb{N},\quad E^{\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}}(n)=\mathbf{f}\,\text{ for }n\in 2\mathbb{N}+1

specify a terminal well-founded induction
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\omega}
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
(in fact, the only one).
Here
\omega
denotes the first infinite ordinal. Since the well-founded model
\mathfrak{A}_{\omega}
is two-valued,
\Phi
is total in
\mathcal{O}
.

The definition
\Phi
is not total in general.
Indeed, let
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
be the
\Phi
-context with
\mathsf{dom}(\Phi)=\{0,1\}
, and with
\mathit{zero}^{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}=0
,
\mathit{succ}^{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}\!(0)=1
and
\mathit{succ}^{\mathcal{O}^{\prime}}\!(1)=1
.
As can be checked, the assignments

\displaystyle E^{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{u},\quad E^{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}(1)=\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle E^{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}(0)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{t},\quad E^{\mathfrak{A}_{1}}(1)=\mathbf{u}

specify a terminal well-founded induction
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\omega}
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
(in fact, the only one).
Indeed,
\mathfrak{A}_{1}
has no
\Phi
-refinements, since
\varphi_{\mathit{Even}(1)}^{\mathfrak{A}_{0}}=\varphi_{\mathit{Even}(1)}^{\mathfrak{A}_{0}[\mathit{Even}(1):\mathbf{f}]}=\mathbf{f}
.
Thus, the well-founded model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is three-valued, and therefore,
\Phi
is non-total in
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
.

The
well-founded satisfaction relation

\models_{\mathrm{wf}}
is a binary relation between two-valued structures
\mathcal{I}
and FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas
\varphi
of which all non-logical symbols are interpreted by
\mathcal{I}
, defined by the following rules:

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}t=s
if
t^{\mathcal{I}}=s^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}P(\bar{t})
if
\bar{t}\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\neg\varphi
if not
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi
;

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi\land\psi
if
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi
and
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\psi
;

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\forall x:\varphi
if
\mathcal{I}[x:a]\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi
for all
a\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})
;

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\Phi
if
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)}
is the well-founded model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}
.

The rules for
\lor
,
\Rightarrow
,
\Leftrightarrow
and
\exists
can de derived from the above rules through reformulation in terms of
\lnot
,
\land
and
\forall
.
An FO(ID)
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
is said to be
valid
or
a model (under the well-founded semantics)
if
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi
for all structures
\mathcal{I}
interpreting the non-logical symbols of
\varphi
.
Two FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas
\varphi
and
\psi
are said to be
equivalent (under the well-founded semantics)
if
\varphi\Leftrightarrow\psi
is valid under the well-founded semantics.

Remark 2.18.

Under the well-founded semantics, every FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition
\Phi
is equivalent to its normalization
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\Phi
iff
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
for all structures
\mathcal{I}
.
This is because the well-founded model of a definition in a context is not defined in terms of the bodies of the individual rules, only in terms of the merged bodies
\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.

A distinctive feature of FO(ID)
\xspace
is that it provides a declarative account of inductive definitions while acknowledging their constructive nature.
FO(ID)
\xspace
differs from the convention
(
Post 1943
;
Spector 1961
;
Moschovakis 1974
;
Aczel 1977
;
Kreisel 1963
;
Feferman 1970
;
Martin-Löf 1971
;
Buchholz et al. 1981
;
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
;
McDowell and Miller 2000
;
Momigliano and Tiu 2004
)
in that it incorporates inductive definitions in its syntax and gives them a truth-conditional treatment.
We claim that the truth-conditional account of inductive definitions in FO(ID)
\xspace
is natural, as it corresponds to the truth-conditional account of non-inductive definitions in FO
\xspace
by means of equivalences.
For instance, the concept of sibling can be defined in FO
\xspace
by the formula
\forall x,y:\mathit{SiblingOf}(x,y)\Leftrightarrow\exists z:\mathit{ParentOf}(z,x)\land\mathit{ParentOf}(z,y)
.
In FO(ID)
\xspace
, this definition can equally be represented as the definition
\{\forall x,y:\mathit{SiblingOf}(x,y)\leftarrow\exists z:\mathit{ParentOf}(z,x)\land\mathit{ParentOf}(z,y)\}
.
In fact, any FO
\xspace
-formula of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Leftrightarrow\varphi
where
\varphi
does not contain
P
is semantically equivalent to the FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition
\{\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\leftarrow\varphi\}
, as can easily be checked.
An advantage of the explicit treatment of definitions in FO(ID)
\xspace
, is that it allows for the expression of statements about definitions
themselves
, instead of only about defined predicates.
For instance, we can express the equivalence of two inductive definitions
\Phi_{1}
and
\Phi_{2}
in FO(ID)
\xspace
as
\Phi_{1}\Leftrightarrow\Phi_{2}
.
The negation
\neg\Phi
of a definition
\Phi
is sensible as well, as it relates to non-totality.
More concretely, if
\lnot\Phi
is a valid, then
\Phi
has no two-valued models.
Hence, its well-founded model in any context is strictly three-valued.
In particular, this implies that
\Phi
is non-total.
More generally, given a formula
\varphi
constraining the parameters of
\Phi
, the FO(ID)
\xspace
-formula
\varphi\Rightarrow\Phi
is valid iff the well-founded model of
\Phi
in any
\Phi
-context satisfying
\varphi
is two-valued.

We conclude this section by providing two properties of the well-founded semantics that will lay the basis of the sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
for FO(ID)
\xspace
.

Proposition 2.19.

Let
\Phi
be a definition,
P
a defined predicate of
\Phi
, and
\bar{y}
an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols not occurring freely in the body
\varphi
of any definitional rule of
\Phi
of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
a (two-valued)
FO
\xspace
-structure such that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\Phi
.
Then
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
be a terminal well-founded induction of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}\coloneq\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\Phi
,
\mathcal{I}
expands the well-founded model
\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
.
Take
\bar{a}\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})^{\mathsf{ar}(P)}
, and let
A
denote the domain atom
P(\bar{a})
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models_{\mathrm{wf}}P(\bar{y})
.
Then the truth of
\varphi_{A}
must have been derived in
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
at a point
i+1
.
By Definition
2.13
, this is only possible if
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}=\mathbf{t}
.
By Remark
2.15
,
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}}=\mathbf{t}
, and hence,
\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.

Suppose conversely that
\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.
Then
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}}=\mathbf{t}
.
By Remark
2.15
,
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}\neq\mathbf{f}
for all
0\leq i\leq\beta
.
Hence, by Definition
2.13
,
A^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}\neq\mathbf{f}
for all
0\leq i\leq\beta
.
By terminality of
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
,
\varphi_{A}^{\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}}=\mathbf{t}
.
Thus,
\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models_{\mathrm{wf}}P(\bar{y})
.
This shows that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.
∎

For monotone definitions, the defined predicates
P
of
\Phi
can be characterized as the smallest relations that respect the definitional rules of
\Phi
, i.e., that satisfy the material implications
\forall\bar{x}:\varphi\Rightarrow P(\bar{t})
corresponding to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
of
\Phi
.
Formally, any model of a monotone definition
\Phi
satisfies the following second-order axiom:

\forall G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}:\Big(\bigwedge\forall\bar{y}:\psi[G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}/\mathrm{def}(\Phi)]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{s})\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\bigwedge\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})\Big)

Here
G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}
is a tuple of second-order variables, containing an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-ary relation variable for every defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
.
The first conjunction ranges over all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:G_{Q}(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
, and the second conjunction over all defined predicates
P
of
\Phi
.
We denote by
\psi[G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}/\mathrm{def}(\Phi)]
the formula obtained from
\psi
by replacing every occurrence of a defined predicate
R
of
\Phi
with
G_{R}
.
The above formula is known as the
induction axiom
.
In words, it says that every tuple of relations
G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}
that respect the definitional rules of
\Phi
must contain the defined predicates
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
In general, the induction axiom does not hold for non-monotone definitions.
One counterexample is given by the non-monotone definition of
\mathit{Even}
from Example
2.6
.
For this definition
\Phi
, the axiom takes the form

\forall G:\Big(G(\mathit{zero})\land\forall n:\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\neg G(n)\Rightarrow G(\mathit{succ}(n))\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\forall n:\mathit{Even}(n)\Rightarrow G(n)\Big).

This formula is not satisfied by the well-founded model
\mathcal{I}
of
\Phi
with the standard interpretation for
\mathit{zero}
,
\mathit{succ}
and
\mathit{Nat}
(and, hence, for
\mathit{Even}
), as can be seen by letting
G
be the set
\{0\}\cup(2\mathbb{N}+1)
.
Indeed, this set respects the definitional rules of
\Phi
, but does not contain the set of even numbers
2\mathbb{N}
.
Nevertheless, we can extend the induction axiom to non-monotone definitions by only replacing positive occurrences of defined predicates
R
by
G_{R}
in the bodies
\psi
.
Generalizing further, we can replace
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
by any subset
\Pi
of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
We also refer to this extended version as the induction axiom.

Definition 2.20.

Let
\Phi
be a definition and
\Pi
a subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
The
induction axiom

\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\Pi)
for
\Phi
and
\Pi
is the second-order axiom

\forall G_{\Pi}:\Big(\bigwedge_{\Pi}\forall\bar{y}:\psi[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{s})\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\bigwedge_{P\in\Pi}\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})\Big).

Here
G_{\Pi}
is a tuple of second-order variables, containing an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-ary relation variable for every predicate
P\in\Pi
.
7
7

7

Since the second-order variables
G_{\Pi}
are not fixed,
the
induction axiom for
\Phi
and
\Pi
is strictly speaking ill-defined.
However, since the exact choice of variables is semantically irrelevant, we speak of
the
induction axiom by abuse of terminology.

The first conjunction ranges over all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:G_{Q}(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
We denote by
\psi[G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}/\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{+}]
the formula obtained from
\psi
by replacing every positive occurrence of a defined predicate
R
of
\Phi
with
G_{R}
.

Example 2.21.

The induction axiom
\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\Pi)
for the even number definition
\Phi
from Example
2.6
and
\Pi=\{\mathit{Even}\}
is of the form

\forall G:\Big(G(\mathit{zero})\land\forall n:\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\neg\mathit{Even}(n)\Rightarrow G(\mathit{succ}(n))\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\forall n:\mathit{Even}(n)\Rightarrow G(n)\Big).

Proposition 2.22.

Let
\Phi
be a definition and
\Pi
a subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be an
FO
\xspace
-structure such that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{wf}}\Phi
.
Then
\mathcal{I}\models\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\Pi)
.

Proof.

See Appendix
A
.
∎

3.
A Sequent Calculus for FO(ID)
\xspace

To enable formal proofs for general inductive definitions, we introduce the
sequent calculus
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
for FO(ID)
\xspace
.
A sequent calculus
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
for a logic
\mathcal{L}\xspace
consists of a number of inference rules, formulated in terms of
sequents
.
An (
\mathcal{L}\xspace
-)
sequent
is an expression of the form
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
where
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are sets of
\mathcal{L}\xspace
-formulas.
8
8

8

In other versions,
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are often seen as multisets or lists.

Unless mentioned otherwise, we assume
\Gamma
and
\Delta
to be finite.
By convention, we usually discard the curly brackets
\{

\}
for the sets
\Gamma
and
\Delta
in a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.
Informally, a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
says that if all formulas in
\Gamma
are true, then some formula in
\Delta
is true.
Hence, a sequent can be seen as a formula
\bigwedge\Gamma\Rightarrow\bigvee\Delta
(if
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite).
Formally, we extend the satisfaction relation
\models_{\mathcal{L}\xspace}
of
\mathcal{L}\xspace
to sequents
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
by letting
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathcal{L}\xspace}\Gamma\vdash\Delta
if
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathcal{L}\xspace}\varphi
for a formula
\varphi\in\Delta
or
\mathcal{I}\not\models_{\mathcal{L}\xspace}\varphi
for a formula
\varphi\in\Gamma
.
An (
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
)-
derivation
of a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
from sequents
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n}
is a finite rooted labeled directed tree where the nodes are labeled with
\mathcal{L}\xspace
-sequents, the root has label
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, the leafs have labels
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n}
, and the edges
respect
the inference rules of
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
.
By the latter, we mean that for every node
n
in the tree, there is an instantiation of an inference rule in
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
such that the label of
n
is the conclusion and the labels of its children are the premises of this instantiation.
An (
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
)-
proof
of a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is an
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
-derivation of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
from sequents
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n}
that occur as the conclusions of instantiations of inference rules in
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
without premises.
The
height
of an (
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
-)proof
T
is the largest distance of a path from the root to a leaf in
T
.
We say that a sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is (
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
)-
provable
or an (
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
)-
theorem
if there exists an
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
-proof of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.
By convention, we write
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
-derivations in the typical sequent calculus style with horizontal bars representing applications of inference rules, which we label with the name of the rule:

(rule)

\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1}
\dots
\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n}
\Gamma\vdash\Delta

The sequents
\Gamma_{1}\vdash\Delta_{1},\dots,\Gamma_{n}\vdash\Delta_{n}
above the bar are the premises of the rule, and the sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
below the bar is the conclusion.

3.1.
A Sequent Calculus for FO
\xspace

Brotherston and Simpson’s sequent calculus LKID
\xspace
for first-order logic with positive inductive definitions extends Gentzen’s original sequent calculus LK
\xspace
for classical first-order logic
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.
Since Gentzen did not consider equality, Brotherston and Simpson complemented LK
\xspace
with two rules for equality.
Figure
1
contains the inference rules for FO
\xspace
as presented by Brotherston and Simpson.
Some rules are only applicable under certain conditions, which are written next to them.
The rule (subst) is only applicable if no quantified object symbol in
\Gamma\cup\Delta
occurs in
t
, by which we mean that if
\Gamma
or
\Delta
contains a formula of the form
\forall y:\varphi
or
\exists y:\varphi
, then
y
does not occur in
t
.
We denote the sequent calculus consisting of these inference rules by SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
.
Every FO
\xspace
-connective comes with both a
left
and
right introduction rule
in SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
, which introduce the connective in a formula on the left and the right side of the sequent in the conclusion, respectively.
We will sometimes apply the rules (subst), (
\forall
L), (
\forall
R), (
\exists
L), (
\exists
R), (
=
L) and (
=
R) directly to tuples of terms
\bar{t}
.
The structural rules (wk) and (subst) are
admissible
in SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
, meaning that their removal does not affect the set of theorems.
In other words, every sequent that is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable with (wk) and (subst) is also provable without (wk) and (subst).
More substantial is the admissibility of the cut rule (cut) in SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
.
This was originally shown by Gentzen for LK
\xspace
,
9
9

9

Gentzen’s proof of cut-elimination for LK
\xspace
extends to SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
.

a result known as his
Hauptsatz
or
cut-elimination theorem

(
Gentzen 1935
)
.
The cut rule formalizes the introduction of a lemma in a proof.
Note that the
cut formula

\varphi
in its premises, which represents the lemma, does not occur in the conclusion.
This is an obstacle for proof search, as
\varphi
can be any FO
\xspace
-formula.
However, the cut-elimination theorem says this rule can always be avoided.
10
10

10

A noteworthy side remark is that eliminating cuts can result in
substantially
larger proofs
(
Boolos 1984
)
.

Structural rules

\Gamma\cap\Delta\neq\emptyset
, or
\bot\in\Gamma
or
\top\in\Delta
(ax)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta

\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\Gamma
,
\Delta^{\prime}\subseteq\Delta
(wk)

\Gamma^{\prime}\vdash\Delta^{\prime}
\Gamma\vdash\Delta

no quantified object symbol in
\Gamma\cup\Delta
occurs in
t

(subst)

\Gamma\vdash\Delta
\Gamma[t/x]\vdash\Delta[t/x]

(cut)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\Delta

Logical rules

(
\lnot
L)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma,\lnot\varphi\vdash\Delta

(
\lnot
R)

\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\lnot\varphi,\Delta

(
\lor
L)

\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\psi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\varphi\lor\psi\vdash\Delta

(
\lor
R)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\psi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\varphi\lor\psi,\Delta

(
\land
L)

\Gamma,\varphi,\psi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\varphi\land\psi\vdash\Delta

(
\land
R)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\psi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\varphi\land\psi,\Delta

(
\Rightarrow
L)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma,\psi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\varphi\Rightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta

(
\Rightarrow
R)

\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\psi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\varphi\Rightarrow\psi,\Delta

(
\Leftrightarrow
L)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\psi,\Delta
\Gamma,\varphi,\psi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\varphi\Leftrightarrow\psi\vdash\Delta

(
\Leftrightarrow
R)

\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\psi,\Delta
\Gamma,\psi\vdash\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\varphi\Leftrightarrow\psi,\Delta

(
\forall
L)

\Gamma,\varphi[t/x]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\forall x:\varphi\vdash\Delta

x\not\in\free(\Gamma\cup\Delta)
(
\forall
R)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\forall x:\varphi,\Delta

x\not\in\free(\Gamma\cup\Delta)
(
\exists
L)

\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\exists x:\varphi\vdash\Delta

(
\exists
R)

\Gamma\vdash\varphi[t/x],\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\exists x:\varphi,\Delta

(
=
L)

\Gamma[s/x,t/y]\vdash\Delta[s/x,t/y]
\Gamma[t/x,s/y],t=s\vdash\Delta[t/x,s/y]

(
=
R)

\Gamma\vdash t=t,\Delta

Figure 1.
Inference rules of SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace

3.2.
Extension to FO(ID)
\xspace

We extend SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
to a sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
that accommodates FO(ID)
\xspace
-definitions, by providing a left and a right introduction rule for defined atoms.
The
right introduction rule for defined atoms
is

(def R)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta

Here
\Phi
is a definition with a definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
, and
\bar{s}
is a tuple of object symbols with the same length as
\bar{x}
.
Intuitively, (def R) allows us to derive heads of definitional rules from bodies.

The
left introduction rule for defined atoms
involves the selection of a subset
\Pi
of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
containing a defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
, as well as an FO
\xspace
-formula
F_{Q}
for every
Q\in\Pi
, called the
induction hypothesis
associated with
Q
.
For every
Q\in\Pi
, we also pick an
\mathsf{ar}(Q)
-tuple
\bar{z}
of distinct object symbols that may occur freely in
F_{Q}
.
We will often denote an induction hypothesis
F_{Q}
by
F_{Q}[\bar{z}]
to indicate that
\bar{z}
is the chosen tuple of object symbols for
Q
.
Given an
\mathsf{ar}(Q)
-tuple of terms
\bar{s}
, we use
F_{Q}[\bar{s}]
as shorthand notation for
F_{Q}[\bar{s}/\bar{z}]
.

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

The
minor premises
consist of all sequents of the form

\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta

where
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
is a definitional rule of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
Here
\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]
denotes the formula obtained from
\psi
by replacing all positive occurrences of atoms
R(\bar{u})
in
\psi
with
R\in\Pi
by
F_{R}[\bar{u}]
.
This inference rule requires that for all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
, the object symbols in
\bar{y}
do not occur freely in
\Gamma
,
\Delta
or
\Phi
.
The premise
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
is called the
major premise
of the rule.
11
11

11

The terms
minor premise
and
major premise
date back to Aristotle’s work on syllogisms
(
Smith 1989
)
.

We also call this inference rule the
induction rule
, as it is based on the
principle of mathematical induction
, in which the elements of inductively defined sets are shown to satisfy a certain property, by finding a suitable induction hypothesis that entails the property, and that is preserved under the definitional rules.
The latter is formalized by the minor premises, and the former by the major premise.

Example 3.1.

Let
\Phi
be the natural number definition from Section
1
.
Suppose we want to prove that a generic natural number
x
satisfies a property expressed by an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
.
Then we can do so with the left introduction rule for defined atoms, picking
\Gamma=\emptyset
and
\Delta=\{\varphi\}
:
12
12

12

Strictly speaking, the first premise should be
\Phi,\top\vdash F_{\mathit{Nat}}(\mathit{zero}),\varphi
.

(def L)

\Phi\vdash F_{\mathit{Nat}}[\mathit{zero}],\varphi
\Phi,F_{\mathit{Nat}}(n)\vdash F_{\mathit{Nat}}[\mathit{succ}(n)],\varphi
\Phi,F_{\mathit{Nat}}[x]\vdash\varphi
\Phi,\mathit{Nat}(x)\vdash\varphi

Here
\Pi=\{\mathit{Nat}\}
, since
\mathit{Nat}
is the only defined predicate of
\Phi
.
We need to find a suitable induction hypothesis
F_{\mathit{Nat}}(x)
.
To satisfy the major premise, this induction hypothesis must entail
\varphi
in all models of
\Phi
.
To satisfy the minor premises, it must respect the definitional rules of
\Phi
in all models of
\Phi
, in the sense that it must hold for
\mathit{zero}
, and it must hold for
\mathit{succ}(n)
if it holds for
n
(or
\varphi
must hold, but that is precisely what we want to prove).
In general, the set
\Gamma
allows adding any number of assumptions, and
\Delta
allows replacing
\varphi
by any disjunction of formulas.
In particular, we can prove that an object is
not
a natural number by picking
\Delta=\emptyset
.

Induction hypotheses in (def L) can be any FO
\xspace
-formula.
However, only certain choices are suitable to derive the premises of the rule.
Finding such induction hypotheses generally requires some creativity.

In the minor premises of (def L), only the positive occurrences of defined predicates are replaced by an induction hypothesis.
Replacing negative occurrences as well would result in an unsound rule:

Example 3.2.

Let (naive def L) be the alternative to (def L) that does not discriminate between positive and negative occurrences of defined predicates, i.e., the minor premises are of the form
\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
instead of
\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
.
Let
\Phi_{E}
be the definition of
\mathit{Even}
from Example
2.6
and let
\Phi_{O}
be the definition

\{\forall n:\mathit{Odd}(\mathit{succ}(n))\leftarrow\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\neg\mathit{Odd}(n)\}.

Using (naive def L), we can derive the sequent
\Phi_{E},\Phi_{O},\mathit{Odd}(x)\vdash\mathit{Even}(x)
, which is clearly invalid:

(naive def L)

(ax)

(def R)

(ax)

\Phi_{E},\Phi_{O},\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(n)\vdash\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(n),\mathit{Even}(x)

\Phi_{E},\Phi_{O},\mathit{Nat}(n)\land\lnot\mathit{Even}(n)\vdash\mathit{Even}(\mathit{succ}(n)),\mathit{Even}(x)

\Phi_{E},\Phi_{O},\mathit{Even}(x)\vdash\mathit{Even}(x)

\Phi_{E},\Phi_{O},\mathit{Odd}(x)\vdash\mathit{Even}(x)

Here we applied (naive def L) to
\Phi_{O}
and
\mathit{Odd}(x)
, with
F_{\mathit{Odd}}[x]\doteq\mathit{Even}(x)
(and
\Pi=\{\mathit{Odd}\}
).

Replacing negative occurrences of defined predicates by induction hypotheses generally yields too strong assumptions.
Intuitively, this can be understood by viewing an induction hypothesis as describing an upper bound on a defined set: it holds for all element of the set, but possibly also for other objects.
Hence, the negation of an induction hypothesis does not necessarily hold for all non-elements of the defined set.
13
13

13

This intuition suggests that, instead of not replacing negative occurrences of defined predicates, we could replace them with formulas describing lower bounds on these predicates.
We come back to this idea in the discussion of future work in Section
9
.

The sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
differs from LKID
\xspace
in the following aspects:

•

LKID
\xspace
does not (need to) differentiate between positive and negative occurrences of defined predicates, as Brotherston and Simpson only consider positive definitions.

•

Sequents in LKID
\xspace
do not contain definitions, as definitions are not seen as formulas in the logic of Brotherston and Simpson.
Instead, definitions are specified separately in their logic, and LKID
\xspace
-provable sequents are valid only in models of these definitions.

•

In LKID
\xspace
, the set
\Pi
in (def L) is fixed as the set of all defined predicates of
\Phi
that are
mutually dependent
with
P
, as will be explained in Section
3.5
.

The following proposition establishes a deductive equivalence between definitions and their normalizations.

Proposition 3.3.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be sets of
FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas and let
\Phi
be a definition.
Let
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
be the normalization of
\Phi
.
Then
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable iff
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.
∎

3.3.
Alternative Inference Rules

Judging from (def R), one may have expected the following left introduction rule for defined atoms:

(def L’)

\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi_{P}(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

While sound, this rule is rather weak.
This is because
\varphi_{P}(\bar{v})
generally contains defined predicates, which need to be introduced by the same rule, often resulting in a circular reasoning:

Example 3.4.

Let
\Phi
be the following definition of a predicate
T/2
in terms of a predicate
E/2
:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\forall x,y:T(x,y)\leftarrow E(x,y)\\
\forall x,y,z:T(x,z)\leftarrow E(x,y)\land T(y,z)\end{array}\right\}

It defines
T
as the transitive closure of
E
.
Suppose we want to prove a sequent of the form
\Gamma,\Phi,T(a,b)\vdash\Delta
with (def L’).
Then we generally need to complete the following derivation:

(def L’)

(
\lor
L)

(
\exists
R)

(
\land
R)

\Gamma,\Phi,E(a,b)\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,E(a,y),T(y,b)\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,E(a,y)\land T(y,b)\vdash\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,\exists y:E(a,y)\land T(y,b)\vdash\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,E(a,b)\lor\exists y:E(a,y)\land T(y,b)\vdash\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,T(a,b)\vdash\Delta

Here we assumed that
y\notin\free(\Gamma\cup\Delta)
.
Hence, to prove the sequent
