\Gamma,\Phi,T(a,b)\vdash\Delta
, we would generally need to prove the sequent
\Gamma,\Phi,E(a,y),T(y,b)\vdash\Delta
, which again has the defined predicate
T
on its left side.

Proposition 3.5.

The inference rule
(def L’)
is admissible in
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.

Proof.

We show that whenever the premise
\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi_{P}(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
of an instantiation of (def R) is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, then so is the conclusion
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
.
Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi_{P}(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Then, by (wk), so is

(1)

\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v}),\varphi_{P}(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta.

Furthermore, the sequent

(2)

\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi_{P}(\bar{v})\vdash P(\bar{v}),\Delta

is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
This follows from results of subsequent sections:
by Proposition
5.5
, the sequent (
2
) is valid under the
Henkin semantics
, and by Theorem
6.1
, this entails that it is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Applying (cut) to (
1
) and (
2
), we find that
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
∎

Another possible left introduction rule for defined atoms would be the following:

(def L”)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta

Here the minor premises are as in (def L).
Following
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
, we opt for (def L) over (def L”), as (def L) enables cut-elimination (for a fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
in our case).
In fact, (def L) can be seen as a combination of (def L’) and a cut on the induction hypothesis, as illustrated in the proof of Proposition
3.6
.
In terms of provability (with cut), the rules (def L) and (def L”) are interchangeable in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, as made precise by the following two propositions:

Proposition 3.6.

The inference rule
(def L”)
is admissible in
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.

Proof.

We show that whenever the premises of an instantiation of (def L”) are SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, then so is the conclusion
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta
.
Suppose that the premises are SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
These are of the form
\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
, where
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
is a definitional rule of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
By (wk), the sequents

(3)

\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta

are SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable for every definitional rule
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
By (ax), so is

(4)

\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta.

Applying (def L) to (
3
) and (
4
), we find that
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}]\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
∎

Proposition 3.7.

Let
SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\textrm{(def L'')}}
\xspace
be the sequent calculus obtained from
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
by replacing
(def L)
with
(def L”)
.
The inference rule
(def L)
is admissible in
SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\textrm{(def L'')}}
\xspace
.

Proof.

The following SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\textrm{(def L'')}}
\xspace
-derivation shows that whenever the premises of an instantiation of (def L) are SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\textrm{(def L'')}}
\xspace
-provable, then so is the conclusion:

(cut)

(wk)

(def L”)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v}),F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

∎

3.4.
Examples

Example 3.8.

Let
\Phi
be the definition of
\mathit{Even}
from Example
2.6
, let
\Gamma
denote the Peano axioms
\forall x,y:\mathit{succ}(x)=\mathit{succ}(y)\Rightarrow x=y
and
\forall x:\mathit{zero}\neq\mathit{succ}(x)
, and let
\mathit{one}\doteq\mathit{succ}(\mathit{zero})
.
We can formally prove that one is not even, given
\Gamma
and
\Phi
:

(
\lnot
R)

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{\mathit{Even}}[\mathit{one}]\vdash
\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Even}(\mathit{one})\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\lnot\mathit{Even}(\mathit{one})

The minor premises take the form
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash F_{\mathit{Even}}[\mathit{zero}]
and
\Gamma,\Phi,\lnot\mathit{Even}(n)\vdash F_{\mathit{Even}}[\mathit{succ}(n)]
.
To complete this proof, we need to choose an induction hypothesis
F_{\mathit{Even}}
that holds for every even number, but not for
1
.
One option is
F_{\mathit{Even}}[z]\doteq\lnot\exists v:z=\mathit{succ}(v)\land\mathit{Even}(v)
, which says that
z
is not the successor of an even number.
The premises can then be proven as follows:

(
\lnot
R)

(
\exists
L)

(
\land
L)

(
\forall
L)

(
\lnot
L)

(ax)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{zero}=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash\mathit{zero}=\mathit{succ}(v)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{zero}\neq\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{zero}=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{zero}=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{zero}=\mathit{succ}(v)\land\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi,\exists v:\mathit{zero}=\mathit{succ}(v)\land\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash F_{\mathit{Even}}[\mathit{zero}]

(
\lnot
R)

(
\lnot
L)

(
\exists
L)

(
\land
L)

(wk)

(
\forall
L)

(
\Rightarrow
L)

(
=
L)

(ax)

(ax)

\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(n)

\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash\mathit{Even}(v)

\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v),n=v\vdash\mathit{Even}(n)

\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v),\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v)\Rightarrow n=v\vdash\mathit{Even}(n)

\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v),\forall x,y:\mathit{succ}(x)=\mathit{succ}(y)\Rightarrow x=y\vdash\mathit{Even}(n)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v),\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash\mathit{Even}(n)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v)\land\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash\mathit{Even}(n)

\Gamma,\Phi,\exists v:\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v)\land\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash\mathit{Even}(n)

\Gamma,\Phi,\lnot\mathit{Even}(n),\exists v:\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(v)\land\mathit{Even}(v)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi,\lnot\mathit{Even}(n)\vdash F_{\mathit{Even}}[\mathit{succ}(n)]

Example 3.9.

Let
\Phi
be the definition of
\mathit{Sat}
from Example
2.7
.
Given
\Phi
and some properties of the construction function
\mathit{not}
, we can formally prove that a structure
\mathcal{J}
containing a propositional symbol
Q
does not satisfy
\lnot Q
.
Let
\Gamma
denote the set of formulas
\mathit{Member}(q,j)
;
\forall f,g:\mathit{not}(f)=\mathit{not}(g)\Rightarrow f=g
;
\forall f,g,h:\mathit{and}(f,g)\neq\mathit{not}(h)
; and
\forall p,i,f:\mathit{Member}(p,i)\Rightarrow p\neq\mathit{not}(f)
.
Then we can prove the sequent
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\lnot\mathit{Sat}(j,\mathit{not}(q))
as follows:

(
\lnot
R)

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{\mathit{Sat}}[j,\mathit{not}(q)]\vdash
\Gamma,\Phi,Sat(j,\mathit{not}(q))\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\lnot\mathit{Sat}(j,\mathit{not}(q))

The minor premises take the following form:

{\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Member}(p,i)\vdash}{F_{\mathit{Sat}}[i,p]}

{\Gamma,\Phi,F_{\mathit{Sat}}(i,f)\land F_{Sat}[i,g]\vdash F_{\mathit{Sat}}[i,\mathit{and}(f,g)]}

\Gamma,\Phi,\lnot\mathit{Sat}(i,f)\vdash F_{\mathit{Sat}}[i,\mathit{not}(f)]

The induction hypothesis
F_{\mathit{Sat}}
needs to hold for every pair
(i,f)
such that
i
satisfies
f
, but not for
(j,\mathit{not}(q))
.
We can pick
F_{\mathit{Sat}}[i,f]\doteq\forall h:f=\mathit{not}(h)\Rightarrow\lnot\mathit{Sat}(i,h)
, which says that if
f
is the negation of a formula
h
, then
h
is not satisfied by
i
.
The major premise is proven as follows:

(
\forall
L)

(
\Rightarrow
L)

(
\lnot
L)

(def R)

(ax)

(
=
R)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\mathit{not}(q)=\mathit{not}(q)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\mathit{Member}(q,j)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\mathit{Sat}(j,q)

\Gamma,\Phi,\lnot\mathit{Sat}(j,q)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{not}(q)=\mathit{not}(q)\Rightarrow\lnot\mathit{Sat}(j,q)\vdash

\Gamma,\Phi,F_{\mathit{Sat}}[j,\mathit{not}(q)]\vdash

The proofs of the minor premises are straightforward, given the formulas in
\Gamma
.

Example 3.10.

Let
\Phi
be the definition of
\mathit{Active}
and
\mathit{ActivePath}
from Example
2.9
.
We can formally prove that if no edge of the form
(a,m)
is ever activated, then all active edges remain active.
We do so by proving the sequent
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\forall n,m,t:\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\Rightarrow\mathit{Active}(n,m,\mathit{succ}(t))
, where
\Gamma=\{\lnot\exists m,t:\mathit{Activate}(a,m,t)\}
:

(
\forall
R)

(
\Rightarrow
R)

(def R)

(
\land
R)

(
\lnot
R)

(ax)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\vdash\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t),\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)\vdash
\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\vdash\lnot\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\vdash\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\land\lnot\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\vdash\mathit{Active}(n,m,\mathit{succ}(t))

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\Rightarrow\mathit{Active}(n,m,\mathit{succ}(t))

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\forall n,m,t:\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\Rightarrow\mathit{Active}(n,m,\mathit{succ}(t))

We can derive the sequent
\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t),\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)\vdash
with (def L), picking
\Pi=\{\mathit{ActivePath}\}
and
F_{\mathit{ActivePath}}[n,m,t]\doteq\exists p:\mathit{Active}(n,p,t)
.
This rule application involves two minor premises:

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Active}(n,m,t)\vdash\exists p:\mathit{Active}(n,p,t)

\Gamma,\Phi,\exists p:\mathit{Active}(a,p,t)\vdash

We can prove this premise with (
\exists
L) and (def L), picking
{\Pi=\{\mathit{Active}\}}
and
{F_{\mathit{Active}}[n,m,t]\doteq\exists s:\mathit{Activate}(n,m,s)}
.
This rule application involves two minor premises:

\Gamma,\Phi,\mathit{Activate}(n,m,t)\land\lnot\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)\vdash\exists s:\mathit{Activate}(n,m,s)

\Gamma,\Phi,(\exists s:\mathit{Activate}(n,m,s))\land\lnot\mathit{ActivePath}(a,b,t)\vdash\exists s:\mathit{Activate}(n,m,s)

and one major premise:

\Gamma,\Phi,\exists s:\mathit{Activate}(a,p,s)\vdash

all of which are proven trivially.

3.5.
The Set
\Pi

Proofs by induction generally involve multiple defined predicates.
In particular, this is the case for mutually defined predicates, such as
\mathit{Even}
and
\mathit{Odd}
in the definition from Example
2.3
.
Proving that every even number is a natural number, for instance, is done by simultaneously proving that every odd number is a natural number.
Formally, this is reflected by the set
\Pi
in (def L), which would be
\{\mathit{Even},\mathit{Odd}\}
in this example.
In general, to derive a sequent
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{t})\vdash\Delta
with (def L) in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, the set
\Pi
can be any subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
containing
P
.
15
15

15

Here we mean that (def L) is sound (w.r.t. the well-founded, stable and Henkin semantics) for any choice of
\Pi
, in the sense that validity of the premises implies validity of the conclusion.
However, one generally needs to choose
\Pi
wisely, as not all choices yield provable premises.

In LKID
\xspace
, the set
\Pi
is fixed to be the set consisting of all defined predicates that are
mutually dependent with

P

(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.

Definition 3.15.

Let
\Phi
be a definition and
P,Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
We say that
P

depends directly on

Q

(w.r.t.
\Phi
)
if
Q
occurs in the body
\varphi
of a definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
of
\Phi
.
The
direct dependency relation

\textrm{DDep}_{\Phi}

(w.r.t.
\Phi
)
contains all pairs of defined predicates
(R,S)
for which
R
depends directly on
S
.
The
(indirect) dependency relation

\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}

(w.r.t.
\Phi
)
is the reflexive-transitive closure of the direct dependency relation
\textrm{DDep}_{\Phi}
w.r.t.
\Phi
.
We say that
P

depends (indirectly) on

Q

(w.r.t.
\Phi
)
if
(P,Q)\in\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}
.
We say that
P
and
Q
are
mutually dependent (w.r.t.
\Phi
)
if
(P,Q)\in\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}
and
(Q,P)\in\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}
.
We denote by
\textrm{MD}_{\Phi}(P)
the set of all defined predicates of
\Phi
that are mutually dependent with
P
w.r.t.
\Phi
.

As demonstrated by Example
3.10
, it is not always necessary to include all mutually dependent predicates in
\Pi
.
In this example, we proved a theorem about the definition
\Phi
of
\mathit{Active}
and
\mathit{ActivePath}
from Example
2.9
.
Even though
\mathit{Active}
and
\mathit{ActivePath}
are mutually dependent w.r.t.
\Phi
, the proof from Example
3.10
applied (def L) once with
\Pi=\{\mathit{Active}\}
and once with
\Pi=\{\mathit{ActivePath}\}
.
Hence, the generality of
\Pi
in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
allows for smaller proofs.
Proposition
3.18
guarantees that we can always restrict
\Pi
to a subset of
\textrm{MD}_{\Phi}(P)
.

Lemma 3.16.

Let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be sets of
FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas,
\varphi
an
FO(ID)
\xspace
-formula,
x
an object symbol and
y
an object symbol that does not occur freely in
\varphi
.
Then
\Gamma\vdash\forall x:\varphi,\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable iff
\Gamma\vdash\forall y:\varphi[y/x],\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, and
\Gamma,\exists x:\varphi\vdash\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable iff
\Gamma,\exists y:\varphi[y/x]\vdash\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

If
\Gamma\vdash\forall x:\varphi,\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, then so is
\Gamma\vdash\forall y:\varphi[y/x],\Delta
, as can be seen from the following SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-derivation:

(cut)

(wk)

(
\forall
R)

(
\forall
L)

(wk)

\Gamma\vdash\forall x:\varphi,\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\forall x:\varphi,\forall y:\varphi[y/x],\Delta

\varphi[y/x]\vdash\varphi[y/x]
\forall x:\varphi\vdash\varphi[y/x]

\forall x:\varphi\vdash\forall y:\varphi[y/x]

\Gamma,\forall x:\varphi\vdash\forall y:\varphi[y/x],\Delta

\Gamma\vdash\forall y:\varphi[y/x],\Delta

In the application of (
\forall
R), we used that
y
does not occur freely in
\varphi
.
The other direction follows by symmetry, as
\varphi[y/x][x/y]\doteq\varphi
.
The proof of the second part of the statement is similar.
∎

Lemma 3.17.

Let
\varphi
,
\psi
and
\chi
be
FO
\xspace
-formulas and
\Gamma
and
\Delta
sets of
FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas.
Let
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
denote a formula obtained from
\chi
by substituting positive occurrences of
\varphi
(not necessarily all or any) by
\psi
, and let
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
denote a formula obtained from
\chi
by substituting negative occurrences of
\varphi
(again, not necessarily all or any) by
\psi
.
If
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\psi,\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, then so are
\Gamma,\chi\vdash\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
and
\Gamma,\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi,\Delta
.

Proof.

See Appendix
B
.
∎

Proposition 3.18.

Let
SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
be the sequent calculus obtained from
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
by restricting
\Pi
in
(def L)
to subsets of
\textrm{MD}_{\Phi}(P)
.
A sequent is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable iff it is
SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

The “if” part is trivial, as every SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-proof is also an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof.
For the “only if” part, we take an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable sequent and show by induction on the height of an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof
T
of the sequent that it is SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable as well.
By Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
, we can replace the rule (def L) by (def L”) in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
.
16
16

16

The proofs of Propositions
3.6
and
3.7
extend to SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
.

The base cases are trivial, and so are most cases in the inductive step.
The only non-trivial case is where the sequent is derived by (def L”):

(def L”)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta

The minor premises

(5)

\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta

correspond to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
Since they admit a smaller SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proofs than
T
, they are SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable by induction hypothesis.
We show that
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta
is SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable by induction over the
dependency order

\prec
on
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, which is defined by
Q\prec R
iff
(R,Q)\in\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}
and
(Q,R)\not\in\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}
for all
Q,R\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
17
17

17

Technically speaking, this relation is a
strict pre-order
, as it is irreflexive, anti-symmetric and transitive.

This means that we prove the statement for
P
under the assumption that it holds for all
Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
such that
Q\prec P
.
Let
\Pi_{P}
denote the set
\Pi\cap\textrm{MD}_{\Phi}(P)
.
We claim that

(6)

\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi_{P}}/\Pi_{P}^{+}]\vdash\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}],\Delta

is SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable for any definitional rule
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi_{P}
.
To show this claim, we start by observing that
\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\doteq\psi[F_{\Pi_{P}\cup\Pi_{\prec P}}/(\Pi_{P}\cup\Pi_{\prec P})^{+}]
.
Indeed, since
Q
and
P
are mutually dependent w.r.t.
\Phi
,
\psi
can only contain predicates
R
for which
(P,R)\in\textrm{Dep}_{\Phi}
.
Now note that
\psi[F_{\Pi_{P}\cup\Pi_{\prec P}}/(\Pi_{P}\cup\Pi_{\prec P})^{+}]
can be obtained from
\psi[F_{\Pi_{P}}/\Pi_{P}^{+}]
by substituting positive occurrences of predicates
R
in
\Pi_{\prec P}
by
F_{R}
(more precisely, those occurrences that were not introduced as a result of the substitution
F_{\Pi_{P}}/\Pi_{P}^{+}
).
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\Phi,R(\bar{v})\vdash F_{R}[\bar{v}],\Delta
is SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable for all
R\in\Pi_{\prec P}
.
By Lemma
3.17
, (
6
) is indeed SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable.
Applying (wk) and (cut) to (
5
) and (
6
), we find that
\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi_{P}}/\Pi_{P}^{+}](\bar{y})\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
is SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable.
Since this holds for any definitional rule
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi_{P}
, and since
\Pi_{P}\subseteq\textrm{MD}_{\Phi}(P)
, we can apply (def L) in SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
to conclude that
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{v}],\Delta
is SC
{}_{{\textrm{FO(ID)}}\xspace}^{\subseteq\textrm{MD}}
\xspace
-provable.
∎

4.
Correspondence to SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace

The introduction rules for defined atoms in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
are based on two first-order properties of FO(ID)
\xspace
-definitions: the right introduction rule (def R) uses the fact that bodies imply heads, and the left introduction rule (def L) uses (a generalization of) the induction principle.
In this section, we make these underlying first-order principles explicit by establishing a correspondence between SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-theorems and FO(ID)
\xspace
-theorems.
In the FO(ID)
\xspace
-theorems, FO(ID)
\xspace
-definitions are replaced by their
first-order approximations
:

Given a definition
\Phi
, we denote by
\textrm{MI}(\Phi)
the set of material implications
\forall\bar{x}:\varphi\Rightarrow P(\bar{t})
corresponding to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
of
\Phi
.
By
\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
, we denote the
induction scheme
corresponding to
\Phi
.
This is the (countably infinite) set of FO
\xspace
-formulas

\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])\Rightarrow(\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}])

where
P
is a defined predicate of
\Phi
,
\Pi
a subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
containing
P
, and
F_{Q}[\bar{y}]
is an FO
\xspace
-formula for every
Q\in\Pi
.
The conjunction
\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])
ranges over all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
The
first-order approximation

\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
of
\Phi
is the set
\textrm{MI}(\Phi)\cup\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
.

As a consequence of Propositions
2.19
and
2.22
,
any definition
\Phi
logically entails its first-order approximation
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
under the well-founded semantics.
Conversely,
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
does not always logically entail
\Phi
.
This is the case even for the positive definition of natural numbers
\Phi_{\mathit{Nat}}
from Section
1
.
Indeed, non-standard models of Peano arithmetic satisfy
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{\mathit{Nat}})
, but not
\Phi_{\mathit{Nat}}
.

Lemma 4.1.

Let
\Phi
be a definition and
\chi\in\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
.
Then
\Phi\vdash\chi
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

Case
:

\chi\in\textrm{MI}(\Phi)
.
Then
\chi
is of the form
\forall\bar{x}:\varphi\Rightarrow P(\bar{t})
for a definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
of
\Phi
.
We can derive
\Phi\vdash\chi
as follows:

(
\forall
R)

(
\Rightarrow
R)

(def R)

(ax)

\Phi,\varphi\vdash\varphi

\Phi,\varphi\vdash P(\bar{t})

\Phi\vdash\varphi\Rightarrow P(\bar{t})

\Phi\vdash\forall\bar{x}:\varphi\Rightarrow P(\bar{t})

Case:

\chi\in\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
.
Then
\chi
is of the form
\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])\Rightarrow(\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}])

for a defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
, a subset
\Pi
of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
containing
P
, and FO
\xspace
-formulas
F_{Q}[\bar{y}]
for all
Q\in\Pi
.
We can derive
\Phi\vdash\chi
as follows:

(
\Rightarrow
R)

(
\forall
R)

(
\Rightarrow
R)

(def L)

(ax)

minor premises
\Phi,\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}]),F_{P}[\bar{x}]\vdash F_{P}[\bar{x}]

\Phi,\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}]),P(\bar{x})\vdash F_{P}[\bar{x}]

\Phi,\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])\vdash P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]

\Phi,\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])\vdash\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]

\Phi\vdash\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])\Rightarrow(\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}])

In the application of (
\forall
R), we assumed that the object symbols
\bar{x}
do not occur freely in
\Phi
or
\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])
, which is justified by Lemma
3.16
.
The minor premises
\Phi,\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}]),\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}]
correspond to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
By repeated application of (
\land
L) and (wk), it suffices to derive
\Phi,\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}]
, which can be done as follows:

(
\forall
L)

(
\Rightarrow
L)

(ax)

(ax)

\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}],F_{Q}[\bar{s}]

\Phi,F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}]

\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}],\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}]

\Phi,\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}]

∎

As with most of the subsequent proof-theoretical results, the correspondence theorem restricts to
regular sequents
.
These are sequents of the form
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
, where
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
are definitions and
\Gamma
and
\Delta
contain only FO
\xspace
-formulas.
Even though non-regular sequents are generally sensible and useful (as illustrated in Examples
3.12
,
3.13
and
3.14
), many statements about inductive definitions are regular, as they are often of the form “given these definitions, the following property holds”.
The correspondence theorem says that the SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provability of a regular sequent
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
comes down to the SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provability of the sequent
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
, obtained from
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
by replacing every definition
\Phi_{i}
by its first-order approximation
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{i})
.
Note that
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is a infinite sequent.
As for finite sequents, an infinite sequent
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is said to be
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
-provable if there exists a (finite)
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
-proof of
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
, for any sequent calculus
\mathcal{SC}
\xspace
.

Theorem 4.2 (Correspondence).

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a regular sequent.
Then
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable iff
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is
SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Then it admits an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof
T
.
We show that
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable by induction on the height of
T
.

In the base case,
T
derives
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
by (ax) or (=R).
This means that
\Gamma\cap\Delta\neq\emptyset
or
\top\in\Delta
or
\bot\in\Gamma
, or if
\Delta
contains an atom of the form
t=t
.
In either case,
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),\Gamma\vdash\Delta
can be proven in SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
by the same inference rule.
For the inductive step, most cases are trivial.
The only non-trivial cases are where
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def R) or by (def L):

Case
:
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def R).
Then the final step in
T
is of the form

(def R)

\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta^{\prime}
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta^{\prime}

where
\Delta^{\prime}\coloneq\Delta\setminus\{P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])\}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta^{\prime}
is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
By the following SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-derivation,
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable as well:

(
\forall
L)

(
\Rightarrow
L)

(ax)

(wk)

\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta^{\prime}
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]\Rightarrow P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta^{\prime}

\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta^{\prime}

Case
:
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def L).
Then the final step in
T
is of the form

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

where
\Gamma^{\prime}\coloneq\Gamma\setminus\{P(\bar{v})\}
.
The minor premises
\Gamma^{\prime},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
correspond to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
By the restriction on (def L), the object symbols in
\bar{y}
do not occur freely in
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
or
\Delta
.
Consequently, they do not occur freely in
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})
.
By induction hypothesis,

(7)

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta

is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable for any definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
, and so is

(8)

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta.

From (
7
), we can derive the sequent
\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
as follows:

(
\forall
R)

(
\Rightarrow
R)

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta

In the application of (
\forall
R), we used that the object symbols in
\bar{y}
do not occur freely in
\Gamma^{\prime}
,
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})
or
\Delta
.
By repeatedly applying (
\land
L) and (wk), we can derive

(9)

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v})\vdash\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}]),\Delta.

From (
8
), we can derive the sequent

(10)

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v}),\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]\vdash\Delta

as follows:

(
\forall
L)

(
\Rightarrow
L)

(wk)

(ax)

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v})\vdash P(\bar{v}),\Delta

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v}),F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v}),P(\bar{v})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta

\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v}),\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]\vdash\Delta

Finally, we can derive
\Gamma^{\prime},\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
by applying (
\Rightarrow
L) to (
9
) and (
10
).
Indeed, this is because
\bigwedge_{\Pi}(\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}])\Rightarrow(\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}])
is an element of
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{i})
for the definition
\Phi_{i}
defining
P
.

Suppose conversely that
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is provable in SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
.
Since proofs are finite, there must be some finite subset
X
of
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1})\cup\dots\cup\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})
such that
\Gamma,X\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
18
18

18

This can be shown by induction on the height of a proof of
\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n}),\Gamma\vdash\Delta
.

Since every inference rule of SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
is also an inference rule of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, it follows that
\Gamma,X\vdash\Delta
is also SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Applying (wk), we find that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},X\vdash\Delta
is provable in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.
Pick some
\chi\in X
.
By Lemma
4.1
and (wk),
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},X\setminus\{\chi\}\vdash\chi,\Delta
is provable in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.
By (cut), we find that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},X\setminus\{\chi\}\vdash\Delta
is also SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable:

(cut)

\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},X\setminus\{\chi\}\vdash\chi,\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},X\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n},X\setminus\{\chi\}\vdash\Delta

Repeating this argument a finite number of times, we obtain that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
∎

The correspondence theorem characterizes the deductive strength of a definition
\Phi
in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
by its first-order approximation
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)=\textrm{MI}(\Phi)\cup\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
.
Note that the induction scheme
\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
corresponding to a definition
\Phi
is generally weaker than the induction axioms
\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\Pi)
from Definition
2.20
for all
\Pi\subseteq\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
This is because the second-order variables in the induction axioms range over
all
relations over the domain, while the induction scheme restricts to the relations defined by FO
\xspace
-formulas.
For an infinite domain
D
, there are uncountably many relations over
D
, but only countably many FO
\xspace
-definable ones.

5.
Alternative Semantics

5.1.
Stable Semantics

The induction rule of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
handles the non-monotonicity of FO(ID)
\xspace
-definitions in a surprisingly simple way:
in the minor premises, only positive occurrences of defined predicates are replaced by an induction hypothesis, whereas negative occurrences remain untouched.
While this may appear as an arbitrary and perhaps naive approach, it emerges naturally from the
stable (model) semantics
for logic programming
(
Gelfond and Lifschitz 1988
)
.
This alternative to the well-founded semantics underlies the paradigm of
answer set programming (ASP)

(
Marek and Truszczyński 1999
)
.
Based on the presentation in
(
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
)
, we will define the stable semantics for FO(ID)
\xspace
,
19
19

19

Even though Denecker and Ternovska do not explicitly define the notion of stable model, they define the notion of
stable operator
, the fixpoints of which are called stable models.

allowing us to capture (fragments of) logic programs under the stable semantics in FO(ID)
\xspace
.
By its non-constructive nature, the stable semantics is not a semantics of inductive definitions.
Furthermore, an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition (or program)
\Phi
generally has multiple stable models in a
\Phi
-context, whereas a definition ought to provide a single interpretation of its defined predicates given an interpretation of its parameters.
Nevertheless, the stable semantics coincides with the well-founded semantics on all ‘sensible’ definitions
\Phi
and
\Phi
-contexts
\mathcal{O}
.
More concretely, if the well-founded model
\mathcal{I}
of a definition
\Phi
in a
\Phi
-context
\mathcal{O}
is two-valued, then
\Phi
has a unique stable model in
\mathcal{O}
, which is precisely
\mathcal{I}
.

Henceforth, we fix an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition
\Phi
and a
\Phi
-context
\mathcal{O}
.
The
structure space

\mathcal{S}
be the set of all
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
-structures expanding
\mathcal{O}
.
For
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{S}
, we write
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
if
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}}
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
As can be easily checked, the relation
\sqsubseteq
defines a
complete lattice order
on
\mathcal{S}
.
This means that any subset
A
of
\mathcal{S}
has a (unique) least upper bound and greatest lower bound, which we denote by
\sqcup A
and
\sqcap A
respectively.
Let
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{\prime}
be a set that contains, for each
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, a predicate symbol
P^{\prime}
with the same arity as
P
that does not occur in
\Phi
.
Given
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{S}
, we define
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}
as the expansion of
\mathcal{I}
with vocabulary
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)\cup\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{\prime}
that interprets every
P^{\prime}
as
P^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
In short,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}=\mathcal{I}[\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{\prime}:\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{\mathcal{J}}]
.
Given an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
, we denote by
\varphi^{\prime}
the formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing all negative occurrences of defined predicates
P
of
\Phi
by
P^{\prime}
.
We define the operator
\mathcal{C}:\mathcal{S}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}
by
P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\coloneq\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{J}}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})^{\prime}\}
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, where
\bar{y}
is an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols not occurring freely in the body
\varphi
of any definitional rule of
\Phi
of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
20
20

20

We only need to define the interpretation of defined predicates, as the interpretation of parameters is fixed by
\mathcal{O}
.

Given
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{S}
, we denote by
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{J})
the unary operator
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{I}\mapsto\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
.
It is straightforward to verify that
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{J})
is
monotone
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{J})\sqsubseteq\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I}_{2},\mathcal{J})
if
\mathcal{I}_{1}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{I}_{2}
for all
\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}
.
By the Knaster-Tarski theorem
(
Tarski 1955
)
, this implies that
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{J})
has a (unique)
least fixpoint

\lfp(\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{J}))
, i.e., an
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
, and
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
for every
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J})
.
Furthermore, this least fixpoint is also the least pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{J})
, i.e., the least
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\sqsubseteq\mathcal{I}
.
We define the
stable operator

\ST:\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}
by

\ST(\mathcal{I})\coloneq\lfp(\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})).

The fixpoints of
\ST
are called the
stable models
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
.
The
stable satisfaction relation

\models_{\mathrm{st}}
is defined analogously to the well-founded satisfaction relation, the only difference being the rule:

•

\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\Phi
if
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)}
is a stable model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}
.

Remark 5.1.

As stable models are not defined in terms of individual definitional rules, but only in terms of merged bodies
\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
, any definition is equivalent to its normalization under the stable semantics.

To make the link between SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
and the stable semantics more explicit, let us unfold what it means for a structure
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{S}
to be a stable model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
.
First,
\mathcal{I}
needs to be a fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
This means that
P^{\mathcal{I}}=P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}=\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})\}
for every
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
In other words,
\mathcal{I}
needs to satisfy
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
for every
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
Second,
\mathcal{I}
needs to be the
least
fixpoint, or equivalently, pre-fixpoint, of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
This means that for every structure
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{S}
, if
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
, then
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
.
The latter means that
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
The former means that
\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{I}}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})^{\prime}\}=P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}}
for every
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
In
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{I}}[\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi^{\prime}_{Q}
, the positive occurrences of defined predicates in
\varphi^{\prime}_{Q}
are interpreted by
\mathcal{J}
, and the negative occurrences by
\mathcal{I}
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
can interpret the defined predicates
P
of
\Phi
as any
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-relation over
\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})
, we can restate the fact that
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
by saying that

(11)

\mathcal{I}\models\forall G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}:\Big(\bigwedge_{Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}\forall\bar{y}:\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})[G_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}/\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{y})\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\bigwedge_{P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})\Big)

From Definition
2.11
, it is not difficult to see that the second-order formula in (
11
) is equivalent to
\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\mathrm{def}(\Phi))
from Definition
2.20
.
Thus, the stable models of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
are characterized precisely by
\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\mathrm{def}(\Phi))
and the equivalences
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.

By the reasoning in the previous paragraph, analogous versions of Propositions
2.19
and
2.22
hold for the stable semantics.
However, we have only argued that an analogous version of
2.22
holds for
\Pi=\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
The result for general
\Pi
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\Phi
implies
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\Phi^{\prime}
if
\Phi^{\prime}
is a
subdefinition
of
\Phi
.
This means that
\Phi^{\prime}
is a subset of
\Phi
such that, if
\Phi^{\prime}
contains a definitional rule of
\Phi
with
P
in the head, it contains
all
definitional rules of
\Phi
with
P
in the head.
We will prove this more generally for the
Henkin semantics
in Section
5.2
.

The well-founded model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
, as defined in Section
2.2
, can also be defined in terms of the stable operator
\ST

(
Denecker and Vennekens 2007
;
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
)
.
This is done by viewing three-valued structures
\mathcal{I}
as pairs
(\mathcal{I}_{l},\mathcal{I}_{u})
of two-valued structures with
\mathcal{I}_{l}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}_{u}
.
Here
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}}=\mathbf{t}
iff
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}_{l}}=\varphi^{\mathcal{I}_{u}}=\mathbf{t}
,
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}}=\mathbf{f}
iff
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}_{l}}=\varphi^{\mathcal{I}_{u}}=\mathbf{f}
, and
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}}=\mathbf{u}
iff
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}_{l}}=\mathbf{f}
and
\varphi^{\mathcal{I}_{u}}=\mathbf{t}
for any formula
\varphi
interpreted by
\mathcal{I}
.
The well-founded model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
corresponds to the
maximal oscillating pair
of
\ST
.
This is the pair
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
consisting of the
\leq_{\mathrm{t}}
-minimal
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{S}
and the
\leq_{\mathrm{t}}
-maximal
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{S}
such that
\ST(\mathcal{I})=\mathcal{J}
and
\ST(\mathcal{J})=\mathcal{I}
.
Equivalently,
\mathcal{I}
is the
\leq_{\mathrm{t}}
-minimal and
\mathcal{J}
the
\leq_{\mathrm{t}}
-maximal fixpoint of
\ST^{2}\coloneq\ST\circ\ST
.
Translating a result by Van Gelder et al.
(
Van Gelder et al. 1991
)
to the logic FO(ID)
\xspace
, Denecker and Ternovska showed that if the well-founded model
\mathcal{I}
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
is two-valued, there is precisely one stable model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
, which is
\mathcal{I}

(
Denecker and Ternovska 2008
)
.
Hence, the well-founded and the stable semantics agree whenever
\Phi
is total in
\mathcal{O}
.

5.2.
Henkin Semantics

We generalize the stable semantics to the
Henkin semantics
for FO(ID)
\xspace
.
This semantics is based on the homonymous semantics for the inductive definition logic of Brotherston and Simpson
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
, which is in turn inspired by Henkin’s semantics for second-order and higher-order logic
(
Henkin 1950
)
.
In the Henkin semantics for FO(ID)
\xspace
, a structure
\mathcal{I}
is supplemented with a
Henkin class

\mathcal{H}
consisting of subsets of
\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})^{k}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The
Henkin models
of a definition
\Phi
are then defined by restricting the structure space
\mathcal{S}
to those
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
-structures that interpret all defined predicates of
\Phi
as relations in
\mathcal{H}
.
The sets in a Henkin class must be sufficiently rich.
Given a set
A
, we denote by
\mathcal{P}(A)
the power set of
A
.

Definition 5.2.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an FO
\xspace
-structure with domain
D
.
A
Henkin class

\mathcal{H}
for
\mathcal{I}
is a set
\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
such that
H_{k}\subseteq\mathcal{P}(D^{k})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and

(i)

\{(a,a)\mid a\in D\}\in H_{2}
;

(ii)

Q^{\mathcal{I}}\in H_{\mathsf{ar}(Q)}
for every predicate symbol
Q
in
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
;

(iii)

if
R\in H_{k+1}
and
a\in D
, then
\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in D^{k}\mid(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a)\in R\}\in H_{k}
;

(iv)

if
R\in H_{k}
,
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
are terms,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
are distinct object symbols and
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\in D
, then

\{\bar{a}\in D^{n}\mid\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\bar{y}:\bar{b}]}\in R\}\in H_{n}
;

(v)

if
R\in H_{k}
, then
D^{k}\setminus R\in H_{k}
;

(vi)

if
R\in H_{k}
and
S\in H_{k}
, then
R\cap S\in H_{k}
;

(vii)

if
R\in H_{k+1}
, then
\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in D^{k}\mid(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a)\in R\text{ for all }a\in D\}\in H_{k}
.

A
Henkin structure
is a pair
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
consisting of an FO
\xspace
-structure
\mathcal{I}
and a Henkin class
\mathcal{H}
for
\mathcal{I}
.

Henkin classes are sufficiently rich to interpret FO
\xspace
-formulas.
This result will be used in establishing the soundness of LFO(ID)
\xspace
w.r.t. the Henkin semantics.

Proposition 5.3.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an
FO
\xspace
-structure with domain
D
and
\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
a Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
.
Let
\varphi
be an
FO
\xspace
-formula over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
,
x_{1},\dots,x_{k},y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
distinct variables and
b_{1},\dots,b_{n}\in D
.
Then

\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}\in H_{k}.

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

Conversely, one can obtain Henkin classes by collecting FO
\xspace
-
definable sets
.

Proposition 5.4.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an
FO
\xspace
-structure with domain
D
.
For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let
H_{k}
be the set of all sets of the
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}

such that
\varphi
is an
FO
\xspace
-formula over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
,
\bar{x}
and
\bar{y}
are tuples of distinct object symbols (not sharing any object symbol) and
\bar{b}
a tuple of elements of
D
with the same length as
\bar{y}
.
Then
\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is a Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
, which we call the
Henkin class of FO
\xspace
-definable sets for
\mathcal{I}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

Henceforth, we fix an FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition
\Phi
and a
\Phi
-context
\mathcal{O}
.
We define the structure space
\mathcal{S}
, the relation
\sqsubseteq
and the operator
\mathcal{C}
as in Section
5.1
.
Let
\mathcal{I}\in\mathcal{S}
, and let
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
be a Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
.
We say that a structure
\mathcal{J}\in\mathcal{S}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-
point
if
P^{\mathcal{J}}\in H_{\mathsf{ar}(P)}
for every
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
We say that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
is a
Henkin model
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
if
\mathcal{I}
is the
least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point
of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
(given that it exists).
This means that
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})
, and that
\mathcal{J}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})
implies
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
for every
\mathcal{H}
-point
\mathcal{J}
.
The
Henkin satisfaction relation

\models_{\mathrm{H}}
in FO(ID)
\xspace
is a relation between Henkin structures and FO(ID)
\xspace
-formulas, defined by the following rules:

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}t=s
if
t^{\mathcal{I}}=s^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}P(\bar{t})
if
\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}}\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi\land\psi
if
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
and
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\psi
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\lnot\varphi
if not
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\forall x:\varphi
if
(\mathcal{I}[x:a],\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
a\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
if
(\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)},\mathcal{H})
is a Henkin model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}
.

As before, the rules for
\lor
,
\Rightarrow
,
\Leftrightarrow
and
\exists
can de derived from the above rules through reformulation in terms of
\lnot
,
\land
and
\forall
.
Note that if
\varphi
is an FO
\xspace
-formula, then
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
comes down to
\mathcal{I}\models\varphi
.
Since the definition of Henkin model does not refer to individual definition rules, any FO(ID)
\xspace
-definition is equivalent to its normalization under the Henkin semantics.

Any stable model
\mathcal{I}
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
corresponds to a Henkin model
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
, where
\mathcal{H}
is the
trivial Henkin class
for
\mathcal{I}
, i.e., the Henkin class
\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
where
H_{k}=\mathcal{P}(D^{k})
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Indeed, if
\mathcal{H}
is the trivial Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
, then the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
comes down to the least (pre-)fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Conversely, there generally exist Henkin models
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
such that
\mathcal{I}
is not a stable model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{O}
.
Brotherston derived the existence of such Henkin models for the natural number definition, using the existence of non-standard models of arithmetic
(
Brotherston 2006
)
.

Proposition 5.5.

Let
\Phi
be a definition,
P
a defined predicate of
\Phi
, and
\bar{y}
an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols not occurring freely in the body
\varphi
of any definitional rule of
\Phi
of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
Let
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
be a Henkin structure such that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
.
Then
\mathcal{I}\models\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
is a Henkin model of
\Phi
,
\mathcal{I}=\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})
.
This implies that
P^{\mathcal{I}}=P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}
.
By definition of
\mathcal{C}
, this means that
\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models P(\bar{x})
iff
\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
for all
\bar{a}
.
Thus,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.
∎

Corollary 5.6.

Let
\Phi
be a definition,
P
a defined predicate of
\Phi
, and
\bar{y}
an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols not occurring freely in the body
\varphi
of any definitional rule of
\Phi
of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be an
FO
\xspace
-structure such that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\Phi
.
Then
\mathcal{I}\models\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.5
by taking
\mathcal{H}
to be the trivial Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
.
∎

Proposition 5.7.

Let
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
be an Henkin structure,
\Phi
a definition and
\Phi^{\prime}
a subdefinition of
\Phi
.
If
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
, then
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi^{\prime}
.

Proof.

See Appendix
C
.
∎

Proposition 5.8.

Let
\Phi
be a definition and
\Pi
a subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be an
FO
\xspace
-structure and
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
a Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
such that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
.
Let
A_{Q}\in H_{\mathsf{ar}(Q)}
for every
Q\in\Pi
.
Then

(12)

\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\Big(\bigwedge_{\Pi}\forall\bar{y}:\psi[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{s})\Big)\Rightarrow\Big(\bigwedge_{P\in\Pi}\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})\Big)

where the first conjunction ranges over all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:G_{Q}(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.

Proof.

We first show that (
12
) holds for
\Pi=\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
, so that
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{y}:\psi[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{s})
for all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:G_{Q}(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
.
By Definition
2.11
, this implies that
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{y}:\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{y})
for all defined predicates
Q
of
\Phi
.
Let
\mathcal{J}\coloneq\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}]
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-point, and since
A_{Q}\in H_{\mathsf{ar}(Q)}
for every
Q\in\Pi
,
\mathcal{J}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-point as well.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{J}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
To show this, take
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, and let
\bar{a}\in P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.
Then
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{I}}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi^{\prime}_{P}(\bar{x})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}_{\mathcal{I}}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]
interprets the positive occurrences of defined predicates of
\Phi
in
\varphi^{\prime}_{P}(\bar{x})
as
\mathcal{J}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]
, and other occurrences of non-logical symbols as
\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]
, this comes down to
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi},\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{x})[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]
(keeping in mind that
\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}
differ only on
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
).
By assumption, this implies that
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi},\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models P(\bar{x})
.
Thus,
\bar{a}\in P^{\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]}=A_{P}=P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
This shows that
P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}}
, and hence, that
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
, we obtain that
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
.
This means that
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}}
or, stated differently, that
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})
for all defined predicates
P
of
\Phi
.
This shows that (
12
) holds for
\Pi=\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.

The statement for general
\Pi
now follows by Proposition
5.7
.
Indeed, let
\Pi
be an arbitrary subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, and consider the subdefinition
\Phi^{\prime}
of
\Phi
consisting of all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:G_{Q}(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
By Proposition
5.7
,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi^{\prime}
, and by what we proved earlier, (
12
) holds for
\Pi=\mathrm{def}(\Phi^{\prime})
.
∎

Corollary 5.9.

Let
\Phi
be a definition and
\Pi
a subset of
\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
Let
\mathcal{I}
be an
FO
\xspace
-structure such that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\Phi
.
Then
\mathcal{I}\models\textrm{Ind}(\Phi,\Pi)
.

Proof.

This follows from Proposition
5.8
by taking
\mathcal{H}
to be the trivial Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
.
∎

6.
Soundness

In this section, we show that our sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is
sound
w.r.t. the well-founded, the stable and the Henkin semantics.
This means that every SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-theorem is valid under all three semantics.
Since SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is sound w.r.t. the well-founded and the stable semantics, it can be used to reason about (fragments of) logic programs under both semantics.

Theorem 6.1 (Soundness).

SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is sound with respect to the well-founded, the stable and the Henkin semantics.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
be an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-theorem.
Then
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
admits an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof
T
.
We prove by induction on the height of
T
that
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
The reasoning for the well-founded and the stable semantics is similar.
We only discuss the cases where
T
derives
\Gamma\vdash\Delta
by (def R) or (def L), as the cases corresponding to the other inference rules are covered by the soundness of SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
:
21
21

21

We refer to
(
Brotherston 2006
)
for an explicit proof of these cases.

Case
:
the final inference in
T
is of the form

(def R)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta

By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
Let
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
be a Henkin structure, and assume that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigwedge\Gamma\land\Phi
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
, this implies that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]\lor\bigvee\Delta
.
If
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]
, then
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{H}}P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])
, by Proposition
5.5
and Definition
2.11
.
If
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\not\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]
, then
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigvee\Delta
.
Thus, in either case,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])\lor\bigvee\Delta
.
This shows that
\bigwedge\Gamma,\Phi\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.

the final inference in
T
is of the form

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

with minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta

for all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
By induction hypothesis, every premise is valid under the Henkin semantics.
Let
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
be a Henkin structure, and assume that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigwedge\Gamma\land\Phi\land P(\bar{v})
.
Assume by contradiction that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\not\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigvee\Delta
.
Since the minor premises are valid under the Henkin semantics, and since, for every definitional rule
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
, none of the object symbols in
\bar{y}
occurs freely in
\Gamma
,
\Delta
or
\Phi
,

(13)

\mathcal{I}\models\forall\bar{y}:\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{s}]

for all definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
in
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
For every
Q\in\Pi
, we define

(14)

A_{Q}\coloneq\{\bar{b}\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})^{\mathsf{ar}(Q)}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})\}.

By Proposition
5.3
,
A_{Q}\in H_{\mathsf{ar}(Q)}
for every
Q\in\Pi
, where
\mathcal{H}=\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
.
By (
14
) we can restate (
13
) as
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{y}:\psi[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G(\bar{s})
.
By Proposition
5.8
,
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})
.
In other words,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}P(\bar{v})
, we find that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}F_{P}[\bar{v}]
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigwedge\Gamma\land\Phi
, and since
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics, this entails that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigvee\Delta
.
However, this contradicts our assumption, showing that
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
∎

The fact that SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is sound with respect to all three semantics provides insights into the limitations of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, as it cannot prove statements on which the semantics disagree.

Example 6.2.

The definition

\Phi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow\lnot Q\\
Q\leftarrow\lnot P\end{array}\right\}

has no two-valued well-founded models, but it has two (two-valued) stable models: one in which
P
is true and
Q
is false, and one in which
P
is false and
Q
is true.
Hence, even though
\vdash\lnot\Phi
is valid under the well-founded semantics, it is not SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, as it is not valid under the stable semantics.

7.
Completeness

The converse property of soundness is
completeness
, which says that any valid formula is provable.
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
is not complete w.r.t. the well-founded or the stable semantics.
This is the case even when restricting to positive definitions, for which both the well-founded and the stable semantics boil down to the standard least fixpoint semantics.
The incompleteness is a consequence of Gödel’s first incompleteness theorem, as SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
admits a canonical theory of the natural numbers, by capturing Peano arithmetic.
More specifically, this theory contains two FO
\xspace
-formulas expressing the unique names axioms for
\mathit{zero}/0
and
\mathit{succ}/1
:

\forall n:\mathit{succ}(n)\neq\mathit{zero},\hskip 59.75078pt\forall n,m:\mathit{succ}(n)=\mathit{succ}(m)\Rightarrow s=m,

a definition and an FO
\xspace
-formula expressing the induction scheme:

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathit{Nat}(\mathit{zero})\\
\forall n:\mathit{Nat}(\mathit{succ}(n))\leftarrow\mathit{Nat}(n)\end{array}\right\},\hskip 59.75078pt\forall n:\mathit{Nat}(n),\hskip 93.89409pt

and four FO
\xspace
-formulas defining the arithmetic operators
\mathit{plus}
and
\mathit{times}
:

\hskip 25.60747pt\forall n:\mathit{plus}(\mathit{succ}(n),\mathit{zero})=\mathit{succ}(n),\hskip 42.67912pt\forall n,m:\mathit{plus}(\mathit{succ}(n),m)=\mathit{succ}(\mathit{plus}(n,m)),\hskip 17.07164pt

\hskip 34.1433pt\forall n:\mathit{times}(\mathit{succ}(n),\mathit{zero})=\mathit{zero},\hskip 51.21495pt\forall n,m:\mathit{times}(\mathit{succ}(n),m)=\mathit{plus}(\mathit{times}(n,m),n).\hskip 8.53581pt

Although completeness w.r.t. the well-founded or the stable semantics is unachievable, we can still get a theoretical grasp on the deductive strength of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
by proving weaker completeness results.
We do so by proving completeness of a propositional fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
w.r.t. the stable semantics, and by proving completeness of a first-order order fragment of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
w.r.t. the Henkin semantics.

7.1.
Propositional Completeness

In Section
4
, we saw that any definition
\Phi
logically entails its first-order approximation
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
under the well-founded semantics, but that
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
does not always logically entail
\Phi
.
By Propositions
5.5
and
5.8
and Corollaries
5.6
and
5.9
, the same is true for the stable and the Henkin semantics.
When restricting to
propositional definitions
, i.e., definitions in which all predicates are
0
-ary,
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
logically entails
\Phi
under the stable semantics.
22
22

22

\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
also logically entails
\Phi
under the Henkin semantics.
The proof is analogous to the proof of Lemma
7.1
.

Lemma 7.1.

Let
\Phi
be a propositional definition.
Then
\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
logically entails
\Phi
under the stable semantics.

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{I}
be an FO
\xspace
-structure such that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
.
Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
, and that
\Phi
is in normal form.
To prove that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\Phi
, we need to show that
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\textrm{MI}(\Phi)
,
\mathcal{I}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Indeed, for any defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
, we have that
P^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}=\varphi_{P}^{\mathcal{I}}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}P^{\mathcal{I}}
, as
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\varphi_{P}\Rightarrow P
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
,
\mathcal{I}
is the
least
pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Indeed, suppose that
\mathcal{J}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
, i.e.,
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
.
Let
P
be a defined predicate of
\Phi
.
For every defined predicate
Q
of
\Phi
, let
G_{Q}\doteq\top
if
\mathcal{J}\models Q
and
G_{Q}\doteq\bot
if
\mathcal{J}\not\models Q
.
Then
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\bigwedge_{Q\in\Pi}(\varphi_{Q}[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q})
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}\bigwedge_{Q\in\Pi}(\varphi_{Q}[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q})\Rightarrow(P\Rightarrow G_{P})
, it follows that
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{st}}P\Rightarrow G_{P}
.
This means that
P^{\mathcal{I}}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
Since
P
was chosen arbitrarily, this shows that
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
.
∎

Theorem 7.2 (Propositional completeness).

Let
\Gamma
and
\Delta
be sets of propositional formulas and
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
propositional definitions.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the stable semantics, then it is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the stable semantics.
By Lemma
7.1
,

\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is valid in FO
\xspace
.
By completeness of SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
, we obtain that
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
By Theorem
4.2
,
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
∎

As shown by Example
6.2
, propositional completeness does not hold under the well-founded semantics.
Indeed, for the definition
\Phi
of this example, the sequent
\Phi\vdash
is valid under the well-founded semantics, but it is not SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable (as it is not valid under the stable semantics).

7.2.
Henkin Completeness

The Henkin completeness result for SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
relies on the following lemma, which provides a relation between the first-order approximations of definitions and Henkin classes of FO
\xspace
-definable sets.

Lemma 7.3.

Let
\Phi
be a definition,
\mathcal{I}
an
FO
\xspace
-structure, and
\mathcal{H}
the Henkin class of
FO
\xspace
-definable sets for
\mathcal{I}
.
If
\mathcal{I}\models\textrm{FO}(\Phi)
, then
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
, and that
\Phi
is in normal form.
We show that
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Trivially,
\mathcal{I}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-point.
The fact that
\mathcal{I}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{I}\models\textrm{MI}(\Phi)
.
Indeed, for any defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
,
\mathcal{I}\models\forall\bar{y}:\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})\Rightarrow P(\bar{y})
, and therefore,
P^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}=\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})\}\subseteq\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}]\models P(\bar{y})\}=P^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Here
\bar{y}
is an
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols not occurring freely in the body of any definitional rule of
\Phi
of the form
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
.
The fact that
\mathcal{I}
is the
least
pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{I}\models\textrm{IS}(\Phi)
.
Indeed, let
\mathcal{J}
be a pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Since
\mathcal{J}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-point, there exists, for every
Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, an FO
\xspace
-formula
F_{Q}
and an
\mathsf{ar}(Q)
tuple of object symbols
\bar{z}
such that
Q^{\mathcal{J}}=\{\bar{b}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{z}:\bar{b}]\models F_{Q}\}
.
For every
Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, the fact that
Q^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq Q^{\mathcal{J}}
can be restated as
\mathcal{I}\models\forall\bar{y}:\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})[F_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}/\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{+}](\bar{y})\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{y}]
.
Since
\mathcal{I}\models\left(\bigwedge_{Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}\forall\bar{y}:\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})[F_{\mathrm{def}(\Phi)}/\mathrm{def}(\Phi)^{+}]\Rightarrow F_{Q}[\bar{y}]\right)\Rightarrow\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]
, this implies that
\mathcal{I}\models\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow F_{P}[\bar{x}]
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
Stated differently,
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}}
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
, which means that
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
.
This shows that
\mathcal{I}
is indeed the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
∎

Theorem 7.4 (Henkin completeness).

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a regular sequent.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics, then it is
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
Then
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is valid in FO
\xspace
.
Indeed, let
\mathcal{I}
be an FO
\xspace
-structure such that
\mathcal{I}\models\bigwedge\Gamma\land\bigwedge\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1})\land\dots\land\bigwedge\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})
.
Let
\mathcal{H}
be the Henkin class of FO
\xspace
-definable sets for
\mathcal{I}
.
By Lemma
7.3
,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigwedge\Gamma\land\Phi_{1}\land\dots\land\Phi_{n}
.
By assumption,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigvee\Delta
, and since
\Delta
contains only FO
\xspace
-formulas,
\mathcal{I}\models_{\mathrm{H}}\bigvee\Delta
.
This shows that
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is indeed valid in FO
\xspace
.
By completeness of SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
, we obtain that
\Gamma,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{1}),\dots,\textrm{FO}(\Phi_{n})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
By Theorem
4.2
,
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
∎

8.
Cut-elimination

Brotherston and Simpson showed that cut-elimination holds for LKID
\xspace
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
, meaning that every LKID
\xspace
-provable sequent can be proven without the cut rule.
From the perspective of proof search, cut-elimination has limited relevance in induction-based proof systems when compared to first-order systems.
This is because, unlike in first-order proof systems, cut-elimination in induction-based systems does not entail the
subformula property
.
This property states that one may restrict to
analytic
inference rules, in which the formulas in the premises are subformulas of the formulas in the conclusion.
In induction-based systems like LKID
\xspace
and SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, the subformula property is averted by the induction rule, as the induction hypotheses in the premises do not appear as subformulas in the conclusion.
Therefore, when following a bottom-up approach (i.e., from conclusion to premises), the induction hypotheses are unconstrained by the conclusion, meaning that they can take the form of any FO
\xspace
-formula.
Cut-elimination is nevertheless meaningful in induction-based systems, as it entails that unconstrained formulas need only occur as induction hypotheses, and not as lemmas
(
Brotherston 2006
)
.

As demonstrated by Example
3.13
, cut-elimination does not hold in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.
Indeed, from a simple inspection of all inference rules of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, one can see that no rule other than (cut) applies to derive the sequent
\left\{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow\neg P\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt\right\}\vdash
.
Three more counterexamples are included below:

Example 8.1.

Let
\Phi=\left\{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow O\lor\neg P\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt\right\}
.
We can prove the sequent
\Phi\vdash O
as follows:

(cut)

(def L)

(
\lor
L)

(
\lnot
L)

(wk)

(
*
)

(
\lnot
R)

(def R)

(
\lor
R)

(ax)

(ax)

(ax)

(wk)

(
*
)

(
\lnot
R)

(def R)

(

R)

(ax)

\Phi,\lnot P\vdash O,\lnot P

\Phi,\lnot P\vdash O\lor\lnot P

\Phi,\lnot P\vdash P

\Phi\vdash P,\lnot\lnot P

\Phi\vdash P

\Phi\vdash P,O

\Phi,O\lor\lnot P\vdash O\lor\lnot P,O

\Phi,O\vdash O

\Phi,\lnot P\vdash O\lor\lnot P

\Phi,\lnot P\vdash P

\Phi\vdash O

We used the induction hypothesis
F_{P}\doteq O\lor\lnot P
in the application of (def L).
The transition indicated by (
