) is completed as the homonymous transition in Example
3.13
.
The sequent
\Phi\vdash O
can only be proven via (cut), as can be checked by a simple inspection of the inference rules of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.

Example 8.2.

Let
\Phi=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow O\\
P\leftarrow\lnot O\end{array}\right\}
.
Then we can prove the sequent
\Phi\vdash P
as follows:

(cut)

(def R)

(ax)

(def R)

(
\neg
R)

(ax)

\Phi,O\vdash O

\Phi\vdash O,\neg O

\Phi\vdash O,P

\Phi,O\vdash O

\Phi,O\vdash P

\Phi\vdash P

The sequent cannot be proven without (cut), as can be checked by going through all inference rules.
Indeed, the only other rule that can derive
\Phi\vdash P
is (def R), either from the premise
\Phi\vdash O
or from the premise
\Phi\vdash\lnot O
.
However, neither of these sequents is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, as neither is valid (under any of the three semantics).

As can be easily checked, the sequent
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash P
is provable without (cut).
This shows that Proposition
3.3
does not extend to provability without cut.

Example 8.3.

Consider the definitions
\Phi_{1}=\left\{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow Q\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt\right\}
and
\Phi_{2}=\left\{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow R\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt\right\}
.
We can prove the sequent
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q\vdash R
as follows:
23
23

23

This example comes from
(
Hou et al. 2007
)
.

(cut)

(def L)

(ax)

(ax)

(def R)

(ax)

\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q\vdash Q,R

\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q\vdash P,R

\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q,R\vdash R

\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q,R\vdash R

\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q,P\vdash R

\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q\vdash R

We used the induction hypothesis
F_{P}\doteq R
in the application of (def L).
As before, a simple inspection of all inference rules of SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
shows that
\Phi_{1},\Phi_{2},Q\vdash R
can only be proven via (cut).

Even though cut-elimination does not hold for general sequents in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, we can identify a fragment of sequents for which it holds.
One option is the fragment of sequents
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
such that
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite sets of FO
\xspace
-formulas, and
\Phi
is a definition in which the body of every definitional rule is a conjunction of atoms.
Cut-elimination holds for this fragment because it corresponds to the logic of inductive definitions considered by Brotherston and Simpson
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.
In Theorem
8.6
, we establish cut-elimination for a slightly broader fragment of FO(ID)
\xspace
-sequents
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
, containing multiple definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
, in which the bodies of the rules may also contain disjunctions.
The definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
must be such that no two definitions
\Phi_{i}
,
\Phi_{j}
define the same predicate, and the defined predicates of a definition
\Phi_{i}
do not occur in a preceding definition
\Phi_{j}
.

Definition 8.4.

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a regular sequent.
We say that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is
canonical
if
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite, and for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,n\}
:

(i)

the body of each definitional rule of
\Phi_{i}
is composed of literals by conjunctions and disjunctions;

(ii)

\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})\cap\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{j})=\emptyset
if
i\neq j
;

(iii)

\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})\cap\mathrm{pars}(\Phi_{j})=\emptyset
if
j<i
.

To prove Theorem
8.6
, we follows the semantic approach by Brotherston and Simpson, who proved cut-elimination for LKID
\xspace
by proving
cut-free Henkin completeness
for LKID
\xspace
.
This means that all sequents that are valid under the Henkin semantics are provable without the cut rule.
Combined with Henkin soundness, cut-free Henkin completeness implies cut-elimination.

We say that an FO(ID)
\xspace
-sequent is
cut-free provable
(in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
) if it admits an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof without (cut).

Theorem 8.5 (Cut-free Henkin completeness).

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent such that no negation occurs in the body of any definitional rule of any definition
\Phi_{i}
.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics, then it is cut-free provable.

The proof of Theorem
8.5
is analogous to the proof of cut-free Henkin completeness for LKID
\xspace
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
, and is included in Appendix
D.1
.
The rough outline of the proof is as follows:

(i)

Assume that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is not cut-free provable.

(ii)

We construct (infinite) sets of FO
\xspace
-formulas
\Gamma_{\omega}
and
\Delta_{\omega}
, extending
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, respectively, such that

\Gamma_{\omega},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{\omega}
is not cut-free provable.

(iii)

We construct a
Henkin counter-model
for
\Gamma_{\omega},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{\omega}
, i.e., a Henkin structure
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
such that
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Gamma_{\omega}
,
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\not\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Delta_{\omega}
.
In particular, we have that
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Gamma
and
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\not\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Delta
.

(iv)

Hence,
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is not valid under the Henkin semantics.
By contraposition, Theorem
8.5
holds.

Theorem 8.6 (Cut-elimination).

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent such that no negation occurs in the body of any definitional rule of any definition
\Phi_{i}
.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is provable, then it is cut-free provable.

Proof.

If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is provable, then it is valid under the Henkin semantics by Theorem
6.1
, whence it is cut-free provable by Theorem
8.5
.
∎

As shown by Example
8.2
, we cannot extend Theorems
8.5
and
8.6
to allow for negation in the bodies of definitional rules, not even in front of parameters.
Nevertheless, we will establish a weakened version of cut-elimination for canonical sequents
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
in which the bodies of the rules of the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
contain negation, as long as they are stratified (Theorem
8.14
).
In this weakened version, cuts are allowed, but only on formulas of the form
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{y})\lor\lnot Q(\bar{y})
such that
Q
appears negatively in the body of a rule of a definition
\Phi_{i}
, and
\bar{y}
is a fixed
\mathsf{ar}(Q)
-tuple of object symbols.
We say that a canonical sequent
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is
provable with elementary cuts
(in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
) if there exists an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof of
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
in which every cut formula is of this form.
This weakened version of cut-elimination, which we call
cut-restriction
, is valuable for proof search, as it narrows the options for a cut formula down to a finite list.
Before we prove the result for stratified definitions, we prove it for definitions in which negation occurs only in front of parameters (Lemma
8.9
).
Our strategy is to transform a sequent
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
with definitions of this form to a semantically equivalent sequent
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
with positive definitions
\Phi^{\mathrm{c}}_{1},\dots,\Phi^{\mathrm{c}}_{n}
by means of auxiliary predicates, and to apply Theorem
8.5
to
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
.

Definition 8.7.

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent.
Suppose that the bodies of the rules of the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
are composed from literals by conjunctions and disjunctions, and that only parameter predicates occur in negative literals.
For every predicate symbol
Q
that occurs negatively in the body of a rule of a definition
\Phi_{i}
, consider a distinct predicate symbol
Q^{\mathrm{c}}
that does not occur in
\Gamma
nor
\Delta
.
24
24

24

Note that such predicate symbol exists, as
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite.

Let
\Omega
denote the set of formulas
\forall\bar{y}:Q^{\mathrm{c}}(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\lnot Q(\bar{y})
such that
Q
occurs negatively in the body of a rule of a definition
\Phi_{i}
.
Here
\bar{y}
is a fixed
\mathsf{ar}(Q)
-tuple of object symbols.
For each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
\Phi_{i}^{\mathrm{c}}
denote the definition obtained from
\Phi_{i}
by replacing all negative literals
\lnot Q(\bar{s})
in the bodies of its definitional rules by
Q^{\mathrm{c}}(\bar{s})
.
We call the sequent
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
the
positive rewriting
of
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
.

Lemma 8.8.

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent.
If the positive rewriting
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
of
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is cut-free provable, then
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is provable with elementary cuts.

Proof.

See Appendix
D.2
.
∎

Lemma 8.9.

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent such that, for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, if a predicate symbol
Q
occurs negatively in the body of a rule of
\Phi
, then
Q\in\mathrm{pars}(\Phi_{i})
.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics, then it is provable with elementary cuts.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
Let
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
be a positive rewriting of
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
.
Then
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
Indeed, let
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
be a Henkin structure such that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Gamma\cup\Omega
and
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}^{\mathrm{c}}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\forall\bar{y}:Q^{\mathrm{c}}(\bar{y})\Leftrightarrow\lnot Q(\bar{y})
,
\mathcal{I}
interprets
Q^{\mathrm{c}}
as the complement of
Q
for every predicate symbol
Q
occurring negatively in the body of a rule of a definition
\Phi_{i}
.
It follows that
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{i}^{\mathrm{c}}}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\mathrm{c}}_{i})})|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}=\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{i}}(\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})},\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})})
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
and every
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\mathrm{c}}_{i})
-structure
\mathcal{J}
expanding
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi^{\mathrm{c}}_{i})}
.
Consequently,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
for every
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
.
Since
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics,
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for some
\varphi\in\Delta
.
This shows that
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
is indeed valid under the Henkin semantics.
By Theorem
8.5
,
\Gamma,\Omega,\Phi_{1}^{\mathrm{c}},\dots,\Phi_{n}^{\mathrm{c}}\vdash\Delta
is cut-free provable, and by Lemma
8.8
,
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is provable with elementary cuts.
∎

We now generalize the previous lemma to canonical sequents with stratified definitions.
We do so by decomposing the stratified definitions into monotone subdefinition.

Definition 8.10.

Let
\Phi
be a stratified definition.
A
decomposition
of
\Phi
is a tuple
(\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{k})
of subdefinitions of
\Phi
such that the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{k}
form a partitioning of
\Phi
, and there exists a stratification
\ell
of
\Phi
such that for all
i,j\in\{1,\dots,k\}
,
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})
and
Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{j})
:
\ell(P)<\ell(Q)
iff
i<j
.
We say that the stratification
\ell

corresponds to
the decomposition
(\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{k})
.

Remark 8.11.

It is straightforward to see that every stratified definition
\Phi
admits a decomposition
(\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{k})
, and that
\ell(P)=\ell(Q)
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
and all
P,Q\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})
, where
\ell
is a stratification of
\Phi
corresponding to the decomposition
(\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{k})
.
Furthermore, only parameter predicates occur negatively in the bodies of rules of definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
.
Indeed, if a defined predicate
Q
of a definition
\Phi_{i}
were to occur negatively in the body
\varphi
of a definitional rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
of
\Phi_{i}
, then, using the fact that
P
and
Q
are both defined by
\Phi_{i}
, we would deduce that
\ell(Q)<\ell(P)=\ell(Q)
; a contradiction.

Lemma 8.12.

Let
\Phi
be a stratified definition,
(\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{k})
a decomposition of
\Phi
, and
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})
a Henkin structure.
If
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
, then
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
.

Proof.

Suppose that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,k\}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
.
We need to show that
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Since
\mathcal{H}
is a Henkin class for
\mathcal{I}
,
\mathcal{I}
is trivially an
\mathcal{H}
-point.
To show that
\mathcal{I}
is a pre-fixed point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
, pick a defined predicate
P
of
\Phi
.
Let
\Phi_{i}
be the positive subdefinition of
\Phi
defining
P
.
Since
\varphi_{P,\Phi}(\bar{y})\doteq\varphi_{P,\Phi_{i}}(\bar{y})
for every
\mathsf{ar}(P)
-tuple of object symbols
\bar{y}
, we have that
P^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}=P^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{i}}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
, it follows that
P^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}=P^{\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{i}}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{I}}
.
This shows that indeed
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})\sqsubseteq\mathcal{I}
.

We now show that
\mathcal{I}
is the
least
pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Let
\mathcal{J}
be a pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Let
P
be a defined predicate of
\Phi
, and let
\Phi_{i}
be the positive subdefinition of
\Phi
that defines
P
.
It is straightforward to check that
\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}
is a pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{i}}(\cdot,\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})})
.
Since
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
, the structure
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi_{i}}(\cdot,\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})})
, which implies that
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}
.
Hence,
P^{\mathcal{I}}=P^{\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi_{i})}}=P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
This shows that
\mathcal{J}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{I}
, and thus, that
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
∎

Theorem 8.13.

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent such that the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
are stratified.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics, then it is provable with elementary cuts.

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is valid under the Henkin semantics.
For each
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
(\Phi_{i,1},\dots,\Phi_{i,k_{i}})
be a decomposition of
\Phi_{i}
.
Consider
\Gamma,\Phi_{1,1},\dots,\Phi_{1,k_{1}},\dots,\Phi_{n,k_{n}}\vdash\Delta
.
By Lemma
8.12
, this sequent is valid under the Henkin semantics.
Furthermore,
\Gamma,\Phi_{1,1},\dots,\Phi_{1,k_{1}},\dots,\Phi_{n,k_{n}}\vdash\Delta
satisfies the conditions of Lemma
8.9
.
Indeed, it is a canonical sequent, as for all
i,i^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,n\}
,
j\in\{1,\dots,k_{i}\}
and
j^{\prime}\in\{1,\dots,k_{i^{\prime}}\}
:

(1)

\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i,j})\cap\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=\emptyset
if
i\neq i^{\prime}
or if
j\neq j^{\prime}
.
If,
i\neq i^{\prime}
, this follows from the fact that
\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})\cap\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i^{\prime}})=\emptyset
, and if
i=i^{\prime}
and
j\neq j^{\prime}
, from the fact that different subdefinitions of a definition define different predicates.

(2)

\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i,j})\cap\mathrm{pars}(\Phi_{i^{\prime},j^{\prime}})=\emptyset
if
i^{\prime}<i
or if
i^{\prime}=i
and
j^{\prime}<j
.
If
i^{\prime}<i
, this follows from the fact that
\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})\cap\mathrm{pars}(\Phi_{i^{\prime}})=\emptyset
, and if
i^{\prime}=i
and
j^{\prime}<j
, from Definition
8.10
.
Indeed, suppose that a defined predicate
P
of
\Phi_{i,j}
occurred as a parameter of
\Phi_{i,j^{\prime}}
for some
j^{\prime}<j
.
Then
P
would occur in the body
\psi
of a definitional rule
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi_{i,j^{\prime}}
.
However, letting
\ell
be a stratification of
\Phi_{i}
corresponding to the decomposition
(\Phi_{i,1},\dots,\Phi_{i,k_{i}})
, this would imply that
\ell(P)\leq\ell(Q)<\ell(P)
; a contradiction.

Furthermore, by Remark
8.11
, only parameter predicates occur negatively in the bodies of rules of definitions
\Phi_{i,j}
.

Thus, Lemma
8.9
applies, which yields that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1,1},\dots,\Phi_{1,k_{1}},\dots,\Phi_{n,k_{n}}\vdash\Delta
is provable with elementary cuts.
By induction on the height of a cut-free proof of
\Gamma,\Phi_{1,1},\dots,\Phi_{1,k_{1}},\dots,\Phi_{n,k_{n}}\vdash\Delta
, it follows that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is also provable with elementary cuts.
Here we used that any application of (def L) w.r.t. a definition
\Phi_{i,j}
is also an application of (def L) w.r.t.
\Phi_{i}
.
∎

Theorem 8.14 (Cut-restriction).

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be a canonical sequent such that the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
are stratified.
If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is provable, then it is provable with elementary cuts.

Proof.

If
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is provable, then it is valid under the Henkin semantics by Theorem
6.1
, whence it is provable with elementary cuts by Theorem
8.13
.
∎

Note that Theorems
8.13
and
8.14
generalize Theorems
8.5
and
8.6
, respectively, since, if the bodies of the rules of the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
contain no negation, then there are no elementary cuts.

The counterexamples from the start of this section leave open the possibility that cut-restriction holds for a broader fragment of sequents than the one described in Theorem
8.14
.
Indeed, it is straightforward to check that the sequents in Examples
3.13
,
8.1
and
8.2
are provable with elementary cuts.
25
25

25

The sequent in Example
8.3
is not provable with elementary cuts (as it does not admit any elementary cuts), which shows that there are still limitations on cut-restriction in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.

Hence, the stratification condition on the definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
in Theorem
8.13
can plausibly be relaxed.
Moreover, it is straightforward to check that the sequent
\Phi\vdash P
from Example
8.2
is cut-free provable if we replace (def R) with the following alternative right introduction rule for defined atoms:
26
26

26

It is not difficult to show that (def R) and (def R’) are interchangeable, i.e., that replacing (def R) by (def R’) does not affect the set of theorems.

(def R’)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi_{P}(\bar{v}),\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash P(\bar{v}),\Delta

or alternatively, that the sequent
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash P
with the normalization
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}=\left\{\hskip-3.0pt\begin{array}[]{l}P\leftarrow O\lor\lnot O\end{array}\hskip-3.0pt\right\}
of
\Phi
is cut-free provable in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
.
This raises the possibility that cut-restriction can be strengthened to cut-elimination when replacing (def R) with (def R’) in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, or when restricting to normal definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
.
Thus, there are still multiple open questions in identifying fragments of FO(ID)
\xspace
-sequents that admit cut-restriction and cut-elimination.

9.
Conclusion

We have introduced a sequent calculus SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
for the logic FO(ID)
\xspace
, thus enabling formal proofs about general inductive definitions.
Remarkably, our sequent calculus supports proofs of non-totality, thereby offering formal tools to demonstrate that certain definitions are ill-formed.
SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
extends the sequent calculus LKID
\xspace
by Brotherston and Simpson, formalizing the principle of mathematical induction
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.
While covering a substantially larger class of inductive definitions, our sequent calculus remains syntactically close to LKID
\xspace
, the most fundamental difference being the asymmetry between positive and negative occurrences of defined atoms in the induction rule.
We motivated our choice for this extended induction rule by linking it to the stable semantics, which is closely related to the well-founded semantics.
To corroborate our calculus, we demonstrated it on various examples and established multiple proof-theoretical results for it.

This paper paves the way for several directions for future research:

•

As remarked at the end of Section
8
, the examples from the start of the section leave open the possibility that cut-restriction holds for a broader fragment than the one described in Theorem
8.14
.
Furthermore, the cut-restriction result for the sequents
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
in this fragment may be strengthened to cut-elimination by replacing (def R) by (def R’) in SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, or by restricting to normal definitions
\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}
.

•

Following the intuition from Section
3
of viewing induction hypotheses as upper bounds on defined predicates, we could refine our induction rule by substituting the negative occurrences of defined predicates with formulas describing a lower bound on these predicates.
Such a rule could be stronger than our current induction rule.
On the other hand, this refined rule would be more complex, requiring the choice of an additional kind of “induction hypothesis”,
it would presumably not be sound w.r.t. the stable semantics, and it would deviate further from the principle of mathematical induction as used in practice.

•

Hou Ping and Denecker enhanced the deductive strength of their sequent calculus for FO(ID)
\xspace
by including least fixpoint expressions, thereby enlarging the class of expressible sets (while also introducing an infinitary inference rule)
(
Hou and Denecker 2009
)
.
One could investigate whether the same principle applies to SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
, as the addition of least fixpoint expressions would enlarge the class of expressible induction hypotheses.

•

Brotherston and Simpson also devised an infinitary and a cyclic sequent calculus for their logic of inductive definitions, in which proofs can take the form of infinite trees and graphs, respectively
(
Brotherston and Simpson 2011
)
.
These calculi can be seen as formalizations of the principle of infinite descent.
Compared to LKID
\xspace
, the main advantage of these calculi is that they do not require the use of induction hypotheses.
Furthermore, both calculi are strictly stronger than LKID
\xspace
, i.e., their set of theorems is a strict superset of the set of LKID
\xspace
-theorems
(
Berardi and Tatsuta 2019
)
.
A straightforward question is whether this infinitary and this cyclic counterpart to LKID
\xspace
can be extended to FO(ID)
\xspace
as well.

•

Even though SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
admits proofs of non-totality, it does not admit proofs of totality of definitions.
Such proofs would nevertheless be valuable, as they show why certain definitions construct well-defined relations.
In fact, enabling proofs of totality would require extending the language of FO(ID)
\xspace
, as it is currently not possible to express that a definition is total in FO(ID)
\xspace
.

•

Besides sequent calculi, one could also develop natural deduction systems for FO(ID)
\xspace
.
Natural deduction systems are typically less susceptible to theoretical analysis than sequent calculi, but their proofs correspond more closely to mathematical practice.

•

One could extend SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
to extensions of FO(ID)
\xspace
with additional language constructs, such as types (or sorts), aggregates, partial functions and modal operators.

•

SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
could be implemented in proof assistants and proof logging algorithms, to enable interactive theorem proving and proof logging in FO(ID)
\xspace
, respectively.

Acknowledgements.

This work was supported by Fonds Wetenschappelijk Onderzoek Vlaanderen (FWO Flanders) under the projects
G0B2221N,
G070521N,
and 11A2R26N.
We thank Bart Bogaerts and Maurice Bruynooghe their insightful comments on this work.

Appendix A
Proof of Proposition
2.22

See
2.22

To prove Proposition
2.22
, we associate with each three-valued structure
\mathfrak{A}
a pair of two-valued structures
(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})
.
Specifically, we define
\mathfrak{A}^{l}
and
\mathfrak{A}^{u}
to have the same vocabulary and domain
D
as
\mathfrak{A}
, the same interpretation of non-predicate symbols, and for each predicate symbol
P
,
P^{\mathfrak{A}^{l}}\coloneq\{\bar{a}\in D^{\mathsf{ar}(P)}\mid P^{\mathfrak{A}}(\bar{a})=\mathbf{t}\}
and
P^{\mathfrak{A}^{u}}\coloneq\{\bar{a}\in D^{\mathsf{ar}(P)}\mid P^{\mathfrak{A}}(\bar{a})=\mathbf{t}\text{ or }P^{\mathfrak{A}}(\bar{a})=\mathbf{u}\}
. Intuitively,
\mathfrak{A}^{l}
and
\mathfrak{A}^{u}
can be seen as two-valued approximations of
\mathfrak{A}
by interpreting all unknown facts as false and as true respectively.
It is straightforward to see that
\mathfrak{A}^{l}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{A}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{A}^{u}
, when viewing
\mathfrak{A}^{l}
and
\mathfrak{A}^{u}
as three-valued structures, and furthermore that
\mathfrak{A}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{B}
if and only if
\mathfrak{A}^{l}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{B}^{l}
and
\mathfrak{B}^{u}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{A}^{u}
for all three-valued structures
\mathfrak{A}
and
\mathfrak{B}
.
We define a satisfaction relation
\models
between pairs
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})
of two-valued structures with the same domain and the same vocabulary, and FO
\xspace
-formulas
\varphi
.
We do so by structural induction on
\varphi
, via the following rules:

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models t=s
if
t^{\mathcal{I}}=s^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models P(\bar{t})
if
\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}}\in P^{\mathcal{I}}
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\lnot\psi
if
(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})\not\models\psi
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\psi\land\chi
if
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\psi
and
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\chi
;

•

(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\forall x:\psi
if
(\mathcal{I}[x:a],\mathcal{J}[x:a])\models\psi
for all
a\in\mathsf{dom}(\mathcal{I})
.

The rules for
\lor
,
\Rightarrow
,
\Leftrightarrow
and
\exists
can de derived from the above rules through reformulation in terms of
\lnot
,
\land
and
\forall
.
Under this definition, all positive occurrences of predicate symbols in
\varphi
are evaluated by
\mathcal{I}
and all negative occurrences by
\mathcal{J}
.
Intuitively, one can read
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\varphi
as saying that
\varphi
is
certainly
true when viewing
\mathcal{I}
as a lower bound and
\mathcal{J}
as an upper bound on what is known (i.e., what is true in
\mathcal{J}
is possibly true and what is false in
\mathcal{J}
is certainly false)
(
Wittocx et al. 2006
)
.
We will write
\varphi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=\mathbf{t}
if
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\models\varphi
, and
\varphi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=\mathbf{f}
if
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})\not\models\varphi
.
For a merged body
\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
, we write
\varphi_{P}^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}(\bar{a})
for
\varphi_{P}^{(\mathcal{I}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}],\mathcal{J}[\bar{y}:\bar{a}])}(\bar{y})
.
In the proof of Proposition
2.22
, we will use the following two properties of this satisfaction relation:

Proposition A.1.

Let
\mathfrak{A}
be a three-valued structure and
(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})
the corresponding pair of two-valued structures. Let
\varphi
be an
FO
\xspace
-formula. If
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{t}\}
, then
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\varphi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\varphi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}
.

Proof.

We show by structural induction on
\varphi
that for all three-valued structures
\mathfrak{A}
, if
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{t}\}
, then
\varphi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\varphi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\varphi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}
:

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
t=s
.
Since
\mathfrak{A}
,
\mathfrak{A}^{l}
and
\mathfrak{A}^{u}
have the same interpretation of terms,
(t=s)^{\mathfrak{A}}=(t=s)^{\mathfrak{A}^{l}}=(t=s)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=(t=s)^{\mathfrak{A}^{u}}=(t=s)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
P(\bar{t})
.
If
P(\bar{t})^{\mathfrak{A}}\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{t}\}
, then
P(\bar{t})^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=P(\bar{t})^{\mathfrak{A}^{l}}=P(\bar{t})^{\mathfrak{A}}
and
P(\bar{t})^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=P(\bar{t})^{\mathfrak{A}^{u}}=P(\bar{t})^{\mathfrak{A}}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\lnot\psi
.
If
(\lnot\psi)^{\mathfrak{A}}\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{t}\}
, then also
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}\in\{\mathbf{f},\mathbf{t}\}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}
.
It follows that
(\lnot\psi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\lnot\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\lnot\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=(\lnot\psi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}
and
(\lnot\psi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\lnot\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\lnot\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=(\lnot\psi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\psi\land\chi
.
If
(\psi\land\chi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
, then
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
or
\chi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
.
It follows that
(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}\land\chi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\mathbf{f}
and
(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}\land\chi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\mathbf{f}
.

If
(\psi\land\chi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
, then
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
and
\chi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\psi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
and
\chi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\chi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\chi^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
.
It follows that
(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}\land\chi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=\mathbf{t}
and
(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}\land\chi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\mathbf{t}
.

Cases
:
\varphi
is of the form
\psi\lor\chi
,
\psi\Rightarrow\chi
, or
\psi\Leftrightarrow\chi
.
These cases are similar to the previous case.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\forall x:\psi
.
Let
D
be the domain of
\mathfrak{A}
.
If
(\forall x:\psi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{f}
, then
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}[x:a]}=\mathbf{f}
for some
a\in D
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{l},\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{u},\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{l})}=\psi^{\mathfrak{A}[x:a]}=\mathbf{f}
.
Note that
\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{l}=\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a]
and
\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{u}=\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a]
, i.e.,
(\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a],\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a])
is the pair of two-valued structures corresponding to
\mathfrak{A}[x:a]
.
Therefore,
\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a],\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a])}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a],\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a])}=\mathbf{f}
.
It follows that
(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\mathbf{f}
.

If
(\forall x:\psi)^{\mathfrak{A}}=\mathbf{t}
, then
\psi^{\mathfrak{A}[x:a]}=\mathbf{t}
for all
a\in D
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{l},\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{u})}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{u},\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{l})}=\psi^{\mathfrak{A}[x:a]}=\mathbf{t}
for all
a\in D
.
Since
\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{l}=\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a]
and
\mathfrak{A}[x:a]^{u}=\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a]
, we have that
\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a],\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a])}=\psi^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u}[x:a],\mathfrak{A}^{l}[x:a])}=\mathbf{t}
for all
a\in D
.
It follows that
(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{l},\mathfrak{A}^{u})}=(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathfrak{A}^{u},\mathfrak{A}^{l})}=\mathbf{t}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\exists x:\psi
.
This case is similar to the previous case.
∎

Proposition A.2.

Let
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
be two-valued structures with the same domain, vocabulary, and interpretation of non-predicate symbols.
Let
\varphi
be an
FO
\xspace
-formula.
If
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}
, then
\varphi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\varphi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
\varphi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\varphi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.

Proof.

We show by structural induction on
\varphi
that
\varphi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\varphi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
\varphi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\varphi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
for all two-valued structures
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
with the same domain, vocabulary, and interpretation of non-predicate symbols, such that
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
t=s
.
Since
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
have the same interpretation of terms,
(t=s)^{\mathcal{I}}=(t=s)^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=(t=s)^{\mathcal{J}}=(t=s)^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
, and hence,
(t=s)^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=(t=s)^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}=(t=s)^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}=(t=s)^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}
.
In particular,
(t=s)^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}(t=s)^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
(t=s)^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}(t=s)^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.

Case

\varphi
is of the form
P(\bar{t})
.
If
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}
, then
P(\bar{t})^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=P(\bar{t})^{\mathcal{I}}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}P(\bar{t})^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}=P(\bar{t})^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
P(\bar{t})^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}=P(\bar{t})^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}P(\bar{t})^{\mathcal{J}}=P(\bar{t})^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\lnot\psi
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\psi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.
It follows that
\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}
and
\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}
.
Using the fact that
(\lnot\chi)^{(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L})}=\lnot\chi^{(\mathcal{L},\mathcal{K})}
for all FO
\xspace
-formulas
\chi
and all pairs of two-valued structures
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{L})
, we deduce that
(\lnot\psi)^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}=\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=(\lnot\psi)^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
and
(\lnot\psi)^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\lnot\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}=(\lnot\psi)^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\psi\land\chi
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
,
\chi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\chi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
,
\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\psi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
and
\chi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\chi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.
Then
(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})},\chi^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}\}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})},\chi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}\}=(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}=\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})},\chi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\}

\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})},\chi^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\}=(\psi\land\chi)^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.

Cases
:
\varphi
is of the form
\psi\lor\chi
,
\psi\Rightarrow\chi
, or
\psi\Leftrightarrow\chi
.
These cases are similar to the previous case.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\forall x:\psi
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}
.
Let
D
be the domain of
\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
.
Then
\mathcal{I}[x:a]\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}^{\prime}[x:a]
and
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}[x:a]\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{J}[x:a]
for all
a\in D
.
By induction hypothesis,
\psi^{(\mathcal{I}[x:a],\mathcal{J}[x:a])}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}[x:a],\mathcal{J}^{\prime}[x:a])}
and
\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime}[x:a],\mathcal{I}^{\prime}[x:a])}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\psi^{(\mathcal{J}[x:a],\mathcal{I}[x:a])}
for all
a\in D
.
It follows that
(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{J})}=\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{I}[x:a],\mathcal{J}[x:a])}\mid a\in D\}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime}[x:a],\mathcal{J}^{\prime}[x:a])}\mid a\in D\}=(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathcal{I}^{\prime},\mathcal{J}^{\prime})}
and
(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}=\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{J}^{\prime}[x:a],\mathcal{I}^{\prime}[x:a])}\mid a\in D\}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\min_{\leq_{\mathrm{t}}}\{\psi^{(\mathcal{J}[x:a],\mathcal{I}[x:a])}\mid a\in D\}=(\forall x:\psi)^{(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\exists x:\psi
.
This case is similar to the previous case.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.22
.

We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
, so that
\mathcal{I}
is the well-founded model of
\Phi
in
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\varphi)}
, and that
\Phi
is normal.
Let
A_{\Pi}
be a tuple of sets
A_{Q}\subseteq D^{\mathsf{ar}(Q)}
for every
Q\in\Pi
, and assume that

(15)

\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{z}:\varphi_{Q}(\bar{z})[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\Rightarrow G_{Q}(\bar{z})

for every
Q\in\Pi
, where
\bar{z}
is an
\mathsf{ar}(Q)
-tuple of object symbols.
We need to show that
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi}]\models\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{x})\Rightarrow G_{P}(\bar{x})
, which comes down to
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq A_{P}
.
Suppose by contradiction that
P^{\mathcal{I}}\not\subseteq A_{P}
.
For every
Q\in\Pi
, let
V_{Q}
be the set
Q^{\mathcal{I}}\setminus A_{Q}
.
Then
V_{P}\neq\emptyset
.
Let
\langle\mathfrak{A}_{i}\rangle_{0\leq i\leq\beta}
be a well-founded induction for
\Phi
in
\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{pars}(\Phi)}
.
Take
i
such that
Q^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{u}
for all
Q\in\Pi
and all
\bar{b}\in V_{Q}
, but
Q^{\mathfrak{A}_{i+1}}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{t}
for some
Q\in\Pi
and some
\bar{b}\in V_{Q}
.
Note that such
i
must exist, since all domain atoms
Q(\bar{b})
with
Q\in\Pi
and
\bar{b}\in V_{Q}
are unknown in
\mathfrak{A}_{0}
, but true in
\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}
, by definition of
V_{Q}
.
Hence, their truth must be derived at a certain step
i+1
.
Furthermore, the fact that
V_{P}
is non-empty guarantees that there is at least one domain atom of this form.
Fix a predicate symbol
Q\in\Pi
and a tuple
\bar{b}\in V_{Q}
for which
Q^{\mathfrak{A}_{i+1}}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{t}
.
In words, the truth of
Q(\bar{b})
gets derived at step
i+1
of the well-founded induction.
This is only possible if
\varphi_{Q(\bar{b})}^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}=\mathbf{t}
.
By Proposition
A.1
, it follows that
\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l},\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{u})}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{t}
.
Furthermore, we have that
\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}],\mathcal{I})}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{f}
.
Indeed,
\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}],\mathcal{I})}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{t}
would mean that
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi},\bar{z}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi_{Q}(\bar{z})[G_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]
,
and by (
15
), this entails
\mathcal{I}[G_{\Pi}:A_{\Pi},\bar{z}:\bar{b}]\models G_{Q}(\bar{z})
.
However, the latter comes down to
\bar{b}\in A_{Q}
, which contradicts
\bar{b}\in V_{Q}
.
Consequently,

(16)

\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}],\mathcal{I})}(\bar{b})=\mathbf{f}<_{\mathrm{t}}\mathbf{t}=\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l},\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{u})}(\bar{b}).

We proceed by observing the inequalities

(17)

\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}]\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{I}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{u}.

The right inequality follows from the fact that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}\leq_{\mathrm{p}}\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}
, and hence
\mathcal{I}=\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{A}_{\beta}^{u}\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{u}
.
Similarly, we find that
\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l}\leq\mathcal{I}
.
Thus, to prove the left inequality, it suffices to show that
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l}}\subseteq R^{\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}]}=A_{R}
for all
R\in\Pi
.
Take
\bar{c}\in R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l}}
.
Then
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}(\bar{c})=\mathbf{t}
.
This means that the truth of
R(\bar{c})
has been derived by step
i
of the well-founded induction.
Hence,
\bar{c}\in R^{\mathcal{I}}
.
This entails that
\bar{c}\in A_{R}
.
Indeed,
\bar{c}\notin A_{R}
would imply that
\bar{c}\in R^{\mathcal{W}}\setminus A_{R}=V_{R}
, but then
R^{\mathfrak{A}_{i}}(\bar{c})=\mathbf{u}\neq\mathbf{t}
by the choice of
i
.
This proves the left equality of (
17
).
By Proposition
A.2
, the inequalities of (
17
) imply that
\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{l},\mathfrak{A}_{i}^{u})}(\bar{b})\leq_{\mathrm{t}}\varphi_{Q}^{(\mathcal{I}[\Pi:A_{\Pi}],\mathcal{I})}(\bar{b})
.
However, this contradicts (
16
).
We can thus conclude that
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq A_{P}
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs of Section
3

See
3.3

Proof.

Suppose that
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
We show that
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable by induction on the height of an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof
T
of
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
.
The base cases are trivial, and so are most cases in the inductive step.
We only discuss the interesting cases, which are those where
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def R) or (def L) in
T
.

Case
:
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def R):

(def R)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta

Then
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
admits an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof of height smaller than
T
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
By application of (
=
R) and (
\land
R), the sequent
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]=\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]\land\varphi[\bar{s}/\bar{x}],\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable as well, and by application of (
\exists
R), so is
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\exists\bar{x}:\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]=\bar{t}\land\varphi,\Delta
.
(Here we assumed that none of the object symbols in
\bar{x}
occur in
\bar{s}
.
We can rename the object symbols
\bar{x}
if this is not the case.)
Since
\exists\bar{x}:\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]=\bar{t}\land\varphi
is one of the disjuncts in
\varphi_{P}(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}])
, repeated application of (wk) and (
\lor
R) yields that
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\varphi_{P}(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Finally, since
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
contains a rule
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\leftarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
, the sequent
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{s}/\bar{x}]),\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable with (def R).

Case
:
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def L):

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

The minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi,\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
correspond to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
The premises admit SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proofs of height smaller than
T
.
By induction hypothesis, the sequents
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
corresponding to the definitional rules
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
are SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, and so is
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
.
Applying (
=
L), (
\land
L), (
\exists
L) and (
\lor
L) to the former sequents, we find that
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},\varphi_{Q}(\bar{s})[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{s}],\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable for every
Q\in\Pi
.
Applying (def L) to these sequents and to
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
, we find that
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable as well.

Suppose conversely that
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
We show that
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable by induction on the height of an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof
T
of
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
.
Again, we restrict to the non-trivial cases, which are those where
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def R) or (def L) in
T
.

Case
:
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def R).
Since the definitional rules of
\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}
are of the form
\forall\bar{y}:P(\bar{y})\leftarrow\varphi_{P}(\bar{y})
, the final inference in
T
is of the form:

(def R)

\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\varphi_{P}(\bar{s}),\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta

Then
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi_{P}(\bar{s}),\Delta
admits an SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proof of height smaller than
T
.
By induction hypothesis,

(18)

\Gamma,\Phi\vdash\varphi_{P}(\bar{s}),\Delta

is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
It suffices to show that

(19)

\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi_{P}(\bar{s})\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta

is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Indeed, if this is the case, then applying (wk) and (cut) to (
18
) and (
19
) yields that
\Gamma,\Phi\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
To show that (
19
) is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, it suffices to show that
\Gamma,\Phi,\exists\bar{x}:\bar{s}=\bar{t}\land\varphi\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta
is SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable for every rule
\forall\bar{x}:P(\bar{t})\leftarrow\varphi
in
\Phi
, using Definition
2.11
and (
\lor
L).
This can be done as follows:

(
\exists
L)

(
\land
L)

(
=
L)

(def R)

(ax)

\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi[\bar{z}/\bar{x}]\vdash\varphi[\bar{z}/\bar{x}],\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi[\bar{z}/\bar{x}]\vdash P(\bar{t}[\bar{z}/\bar{x}]),\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,\bar{s}=\bar{t}[\bar{z}/\bar{x}],\varphi[\bar{z}/\bar{x}]\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,\bar{s}=\bar{t}[\bar{z}/\bar{x}]\land\varphi[\bar{z}/\bar{x}]\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta

\Gamma,\Phi,\exists\bar{x}:\bar{s}=\bar{t}\land\varphi\vdash P(\bar{s}),\Delta

Here
\bar{z}
is a tuple of object symbols that do not occur freely in
\Gamma
,
\Delta
or
\Phi
, with the same length as
\bar{x}
.
(We again assumed that none of the object symbols in
\bar{x}
occur in
\bar{s}
.
We can rename
\bar{x}
if this is not the case.)

Case
:
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}}\vdash\Delta
is derived by (def L):

(def L)

minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta

The minor premises
\Gamma,\Phi^{\mathrm{N}},\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{y}],\Delta
correspond to the defined predicates
Q\in\Pi
.
The premises admit SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-proofs of height smaller than
T
.
By induction hypothesis, the sequents

(20)

\Gamma,\Phi,\varphi_{Q}(\bar{y})[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash F_{Q}[\bar{y}],\Delta

corresponding to the predicates
Q\in\Pi
are SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-provable, and so is

(21)

\Gamma,\Phi,F_{P}[\bar{v}]\vdash\Delta.

Let
\forall\bar{y}:Q(\bar{s})\leftarrow\psi
be a definitional rule of
\Phi
with
Q\in\Pi
.
Consider the proof:

(
\exists
R)

(
\land
R)

(ax)

(
=
R)

\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash\bar{s}=\bar{s}

\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]

\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash\bar{s}=\bar{s}\land\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]

\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash\exists\bar{y}:\bar{s}=\bar{s}\land\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]

Repeatedly applying (wk) and (
\lor
R) to
\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]\vdash\exists\bar{y}:\bar{s}=\bar{s}\land\psi[F_{\Pi}/\Pi^{+}]
, we find that

), we also find that

\xspace
\xspace

) and (

\xspace
\xspace
-provable.
Finally, applying (def L) to (

and to (

), we find that
\Gamma,\Phi,P(\bar{v})\vdash\Delta
is SC

\xspace
\xspace
-provable.

See
3.17

Proof.

We show the statement by structural induction on
\chi
.

Case
:

\chi
is an atom.
Then
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\doteq\chi
, and hence
\Gamma,\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi,\Delta
can be derived by (ax).
If
\chi\doteq\varphi
, then
\Gamma,\chi\vdash\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
comes down to
\Gamma,\varphi\vdash\psi,\Delta
, which is provable by assumption.
Otherwise,
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]\doteq\chi
, and
\Gamma,\chi\vdash\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
can be derived by (ax).

Case
:

\chi
is of the form
\lnot\chi^{\prime}
.
Then
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
is of the form
\lnot\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
obtained from
\chi^{\prime}
by replacing negative occurrences of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi^{\prime},\Delta
is provable.
We can then derive
\Gamma,\chi\vdash\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
as follows:

(
\lnot
L)

(
\lnot
R)

\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi^{\prime},\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\lnot\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}],\chi^{\prime},\Delta

\Gamma,\lnot\chi^{\prime}\vdash\lnot\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}],\Delta

The formula
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
is of the form
\lnot\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
obtained from
\chi^{\prime}
by replacing positive occurrences of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}\vdash\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
is provable.
We can then derive
\Gamma,\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi,\Delta
as follows:

(
\lnot
L)

(
\lnot
R)

\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}\vdash\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
\Gamma\vdash\lnot\chi^{\prime},\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

\Gamma,\lnot\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]\vdash\lnot\chi^{\prime},\Delta

Case
:

\chi
is of the form
\chi_{1}\land\chi_{2}
.
Then
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
is of the form
\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]\land\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
for FO
\xspace
-formulas
\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
and
\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
obtained from
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, respectively, by replacing positive occurrences of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\chi_{1}\vdash\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
and
\Gamma,\chi_{2}\vdash\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
are provable.
We can then derive
\Gamma,\chi\vdash\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
as follows:

(
\land
L)

(
\land
R)

(wk)

(wk)

\Gamma,\chi_{1}\vdash\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
\Gamma,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\vdash\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

\Gamma,\chi_{2}\vdash\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
\Gamma,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\vdash\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

\Gamma,\chi_{1},\chi_{2}\vdash\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]\land\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

\Gamma,\chi_{1}\land\chi_{2}\vdash\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]\land\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

The formula
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
is of the form
\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\land\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
for FO
\xspace
-formulas
\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
and
\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
obtained from
\chi_{1}
and
\chi_{2}
, respectively, by replacing negative occurrences of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{1},\Delta
and
\Gamma,\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{2},\Delta
are provable.
We can then derive
\Gamma,\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi,\Delta
as follows:

(
\land
L)

(
\land
R)

(wk)

(wk)

\Gamma,\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{1},\Delta
\Gamma,\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}],\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{1},\Delta

\Gamma,\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{2},\Delta
\Gamma,\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}],\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{2},\Delta

\Gamma,\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}],\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{1}\land\chi_{2},\Delta

\Gamma,\chi_{1}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\land\chi_{2}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi_{1}\land\chi_{2},\Delta

Cases
:

\chi
is of the form
\chi_{1}\lor\chi_{2}
,
\chi_{1}\Rightarrow\chi_{2}
or
\chi_{1}\Leftrightarrow\chi_{2}
.
These cases are similar to the previous case.

Case
:

\chi
is of the form
\forall x:\chi^{\prime}
.
Then
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
is of the form
\forall x:\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
obtained from
\chi^{\prime}
by replacing positive occurrences of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}\vdash\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
is provable.
We can then derive
\Gamma,\chi\vdash\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
as follows:

(
\forall
R)

(
\forall
L)

\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}\vdash\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta
\Gamma,\forall x:\chi^{\prime}\vdash\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

\Gamma,\forall x:\chi^{\prime}\vdash\forall x:\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}],\Delta

In the application of (
\forall
R), we assumed that
x
does not occur freely in
\Gamma
or
\Delta
, which is justified by Lemma
3.16
.
Indeed, if necessary, we can replace
x
in
\forall x:\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{+}]
by an object symbol
y
that does not occur freely in
\varphi
,
\Gamma
or
\Delta
(and such object symbol exists, as
\Gamma
and
\Delta
are finite).

The formula
\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
is of the form
\forall x:\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]
obtained from
\chi^{\prime}
by replacing negative occurrences of
\varphi
by
\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi^{\prime},\Delta
is provable.
We can then derive
\Gamma,\chi[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi,\Delta
as follows:

(
\forall
R)

(
\forall
L)

\Gamma,\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi^{\prime},\Delta
\Gamma,\forall x:\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\chi^{\prime},\Delta

\Gamma,\forall x:\chi^{\prime}[\psi\sslash\varphi^{-}]\vdash\forall x:\chi^{\prime},\Delta

Again, we assumed that
x
does not occur freely in
\varphi
,
\Gamma
or
\Delta
, which is justified by Lemma
3.16
.

Case
:

\chi
is of the form
\exists x:\chi^{\prime}
.
This case is similar to the previous case.
∎

Appendix C
Proofs of Section
5

See
5.3

Proof.

We prove by structural induction on
\varphi
that
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}\in H_{k}
for all tuples
\bar{x}=(x_{1},\dots,x_{k})
and
\bar{y}=(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
of distinct object symbols (not sharing any object symbol) and all
\bar{b}=(b_{1},\dots,b_{n})\in D^{n}
:

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
t=s
.
By condition
(i)
of Definition
5.2
,
R\coloneq\{(a,a)\mid a\in D\}\in H_{2}
.
By condition
(iv)
,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models t=s\}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid(t^{\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]},s^{\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]})\in R\}\in H_{k}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
Q(\bar{t})
.
Let
k
denote the arity of
Q
.
By condition
(ii)
,
Q^{\mathcal{I}}\in H_{k}
.
By condition
(iv)
,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models Q(\bar{t})\}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]}\in Q^{\mathcal{I}}\}\in H_{k}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\neg\psi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi\}\in H_{k}
.
By condition
(v)
,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\neg\psi\}=D^{k}\setminus\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi\}\in H_{k}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\psi\land\chi
.
By induction hypothesis,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi\}\in H_{k}
and
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\chi\}\in H_{k}
.
By condition
(vi)
,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi\land\chi\}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi\}\cap\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi\}\in H_{k}
.

Cases
:
\varphi
is of the form
\psi\lor\chi
,
\psi\Rightarrow\chi
or
\psi\Leftrightarrow\chi
.
Using the fact that
\psi\lor\chi
is FO
\xspace
-equivalent to
\neg(\neg\psi\land\neg\chi)
,
\psi\Rightarrow\chi
to
\neg\psi\lor\chi
, and
\psi\Leftrightarrow\chi
to
(\psi\Rightarrow\chi)\land(\chi\Rightarrow\psi)
, these cases follow from the previous cases.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\forall x:\psi
.
Let
z
be an object symbol that does not occur in
\bar{x}
or
\bar{y}
.
By induction hypothesis,
R\coloneq\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a)\in D^{k+1}\mid\mathcal{I}[x_{1}:a_{1},\dots,x_{k}:a_{k},\,z:a,\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\psi[z/x]\}\in H_{k+1}
.
By condition
(vii)
,
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\forall x:\psi\}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\forall z:\psi[z/x]\}=\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in D^{k}\mid(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a)\in R\text{ for all }a\in D\}\in H_{k}
.

Case
:
\varphi
is of the form
\exists x:\psi
.
Using the fact that
\exists x:\psi
is FO
\xspace
-equivalent to
\neg\forall x:\neg\psi
, this follows from the previous cases.
∎

See
5.4

Proof.

We need to check that
\{H_{k}\mid k\in\mathbb{N}\}
fulfills all the conditions of Definition
5.2
:

(i)

\{(a,a)\mid a\in D\}=\{(a_{1},a_{2})\in D^{2}\mid\mathcal{I}[x_{1}:a_{1},x_{2}:a_{2}]\models x_{1}=x_{2}\}\in H_{2}
.

(ii)

Let
Q
be a predicate symbol in
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
with arity
k
.
Then
Q^{\mathcal{I}}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models Q(\bar{x})\}\in H_{k}
.

(iii)

Let
R\in H_{k+1}
and
a\in D
.
By definition of
H_{k+1}
, the set
R
is of the form
\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k+1})\in D^{k+1}\mid\mathcal{I}[x_{1}:a_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}:a_{k+1},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
, distinct object symbols
x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1},\bar{y}
, and a tuple
\bar{b}\in D^{n}
.
Let
z
be an object symbol not in
x_{1},\dots,x_{k+1},\bar{y}
.
Since
\varphi[z/x_{k+1}]
is an FO
\xspace
-formula,
\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in D^{k}\mid(a_{1},\dots,a_{k},a)\in R\}=\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k})\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[x_{1}:a_{1},\dots,x_{k}:a_{k},\,z:a,\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi[z/x_{k+1}]\}\in H_{k}
.

(iv)

Let
R\in H_{k}
,
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
terms,
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
distinct object symbols, and
b_{1},\dots,b_{m}\in D
.
Then
R
is of the form
\{\bar{c}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{z}:\bar{c},\,\bar{w}:\bar{d}]\models\varphi\}
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
, distinct object symbols
\bar{z},\bar{w}
, and a tuple
\bar{d}\in D^{p}
.
We can assume without loss of generality that none of the object symbols in
\bar{w}
occurs in
x_{1},\dots,x_{n},y_{1},\dots,y_{m}
or in one of the terms
t_{1},\dots,t_{k}
.
(Otherwise, we can rename object symbols.)
Then
\{\bar{a}\in D^{n}\mid\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\bar{y}:\bar{b}]}\in R\}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{n}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{z}:\bar{t}^{\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]},\bar{w}:\bar{d}]\models\varphi\}=\{\bar{a}\in D^{n}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b},\,\bar{w}:\bar{d}]\models\varphi[\bar{t}/\bar{z}]\}\in H_{n}
.

(v)

Let
R\in H_{k}
.
Then
R
is of the form
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
, distinct object symbols
\bar{x},\bar{y}
, and a tuple
\bar{b}\in D^{n}
.
Then
D^{k}\setminus R=\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\,\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\neg\varphi\}\in H_{k}
.

(vi)

Let
R\in H_{k}
and
S\in H_{k}
.
Then
R
is of the form
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}
for an FO
\xspace
-formula
\varphi
over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
, distinct object symbols

, and
S
is of the form
\{\bar{a}\in D^{k}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\bar{z}:\bar{c}]\models\psi\}
for an FO
\xspace

\psi
over
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})
, distinct object symbols

, and a tuple
\bar{c}\in D^{m}
.
We can assume without loss of generality that none of the object symbols in
\bar{y}
occurs in
\bar{z}
.
(Otherwise, we can rename object symbols.)
Then
R\cap S=\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a},\bar{y}:\bar{b},\bar{z}:\bar{c}]\models\varphi\land\psi\}\in H_{k}
.

(vii)

Let
R\in H_{k+1}
.
Then
R

\{(a_{1},\dots,a_{k+1})\in D^{k+1}\mid\mathcal{I}[x_{1}:a_{1},\dots,x_{k+1}:a_{k+1},\bar{y}:\bar{b}]\models\varphi\}

See
5.7

Proof.

We can assume without loss of generality that
\mathsf{voc}(\mathcal{I})=\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
.
Suppose that
(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{H})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi
.
Then
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Let
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\coloneq\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})}
.
We need to show that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})\subseteq\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
,
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-point.
Since
\mathcal{I}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
,
P^{\mathcal{I}}\subseteq\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{x})\}
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.
It follows that
P^{\mathcal{I}^{\prime}}\subseteq\{\bar{a}\mid\mathcal{I}^{\prime}[\bar{x}:\bar{a}]\models\varphi_{P}(\bar{x})\}
for all
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi^{\prime})
, which means that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
.
27
27

27

Strictly speaking, we use two different operators
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{\prime}}
, defined on two different structure spaces
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}
and
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi^{\prime}}
, corresponding to two different definitions
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
.
However, since
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{\prime}}(\mathcal{I}_{1}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})},\mathcal{I}_{2}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})})=\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}(\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2})|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})}
for all
\mathcal{I}_{1},\mathcal{I}_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}
, we simplify both
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi}
and
\mathcal{C}_{\Phi^{\prime}}
to
\mathcal{C}
, by abuse of notation.

Let
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
be a pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
.
Consider the
\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)
-structure
\mathcal{J}
that interprets any
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi^{\prime})
as
P^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}\cap P^{\mathcal{I}}
, and any
\sigma\in\mathrm{sym}(\Phi)\setminus\mathrm{def}(\Phi^{\prime})
as
\sigma^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Note that
\mathcal{J}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{I}
and
\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
.
We will show that
\mathcal{J}
is a pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.
Since
\mathcal{I}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
, this will imply that
\mathcal{I}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}
, and hence, that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}=\mathcal{I}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
, showing that
\mathcal{I}^{\prime}
is the least pre-fixed
\mathcal{H}
-point of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I}^{\prime})
.
The fact that
\mathcal{J}
is an
\mathcal{H}
-point follows from the fact that
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{I}
are
\mathcal{H}
-points, using condition
(vi)
of Definition
5.2
.
To show that
\mathcal{J}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
, take
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi)
.

Case
:
P\notin\mathrm{def}(\Phi^{\prime})
.
Then
P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{I}}=P^{\mathcal{J}}
, using the fact that
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
is monotone, and that
\mathcal{I}
is a pre-fixpoint of
\mathcal{C}(\cdot,\mathcal{I})
.

Case
:
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi^{\prime})
.
Similarly to the previous case, we find that
P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{I},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{I}}
.
Since
\mathcal{J}|_{\mathrm{sym}(\Phi^{\prime})}\sqsubseteq\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
, we have that
P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}
.
Combining these facts, we find that
P^{\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{J},\mathcal{I})}\subseteq P^{\mathcal{I}}\cap P^{\mathcal{J}^{\prime}}=P^{\mathcal{J}}
.
∎

Appendix D
Proofs of Section
8

D.1.
Proof of Theorem
8.5

We prove Theorem
8.5
:

See
8.5

Let
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
be as above.
We will prove Theorem
8.5
by contraposition, i.e., we will show that if
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is not cut-free provable, then it is not valid under the Henkin semantics.
Assume that
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
is not cut-free provable.
We will construct a
limit sequent

\Gamma_{\omega},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{\omega}
, where
\Gamma_{\omega}
and
\Delta_{\omega}
are infinite sets of FO
\xspace
-formulas, containing
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, respectively, such that
\Gamma_{\omega},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{\omega}
is not cut-free provable.
Next, we will construct a
Henkin counter-model
for
\Gamma_{\omega},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{\omega}
, i.e., a Henkin structure
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})
such that
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Gamma_{\omega}
,
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\models_{\mathrm{H}}\Phi_{i}
for all
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, but
(\mathcal{I}_{\omega},\mathcal{H}_{\omega})\not\models_{\mathrm{H}}\varphi
for all
\varphi\in\Delta_{\omega}
.

Let
\Sigma
be the vocabulary consisting of all non-logical symbols in
\Gamma,\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta
, and let
\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)
be the set of all terms built from function symbols in
\Sigma
.
We construct the limit sequent
\Gamma_{\omega},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{\omega}
via a
schedule
.

Definition D.1.

A
schedule element
is defined by the following rules:

•

If
\varphi
and
\psi
are FO
\xspace
-formulas over
\Sigma
, then
\lnot\varphi
,
\varphi\land\psi
,
\varphi\lor\psi
,
\varphi\Rightarrow\psi
and
\varphi\Leftrightarrow\psi
are schedule elements.

•

If
\varphi
is an FO
\xspace
-formula over
\Sigma
,
x
an object symbol in
\Sigma
, and
t\in\textrm{Terms}(\Sigma)
, then
\langle\forall x:\varphi,\,t\rangle
and
\langle\exists x:\varphi,\,t\rangle
are schedule elements.

•

If
P(\bar{t})
is an atom over
\Sigma
such that
P\in\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})
for some
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, and
F_{\Pi}
a tuple of induction hypotheses over
\Sigma
for the predicates in
\Pi=\mathrm{def}(\Phi_{i})
, then
\langle P(\bar{t}),F_{\Pi}\rangle
is a schedule element.

A
schedule

(e_{i})_{i}
is a sequence of schedule elements in which every schedule element appears infinitely often.

Henceforth, we fix a schedule
(e_{i})_{i}
, whose existence follows from the fact that there are countably many schedule elements.

For each
i\in\mathbb{N}
, we construct finite sets
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
of FO
\xspace
-formulas over
\Sigma
such that:

•

\Gamma_{i}\subseteq\Gamma_{i+1}
and
\Delta_{i}\subseteq\Delta_{i+1}
;

•

the sequent
\Gamma_{i},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{i}
is not cut-free provable.

We do so by induction on
i
:

For
i=0
, we can simply pick
\Gamma=\Gamma_{0}
and
\Delta=\Delta_{0}
.

Suppose we have constructed the sets
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
.
We construct
\Gamma_{i+1}
and
\Delta_{i+1}
based on the
i
-th schedule element
e_{i}
of
(e_{i})_{i}
.
Note that
\Gamma_{i}
and
\Delta_{i}
cannot have any formula in common, for otherwise
\Gamma_{i},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{i}
would be cut-free provable via (ax).

•

e_{i}
is of the form
\lnot\varphi
.
If
\lnot\varphi\not\in\Gamma_{i}\cup\Delta_{i}
, then let
\Gamma_{i+1}\coloneq\Gamma_{i}
and
\Delta_{i+1}\coloneq\Delta_{i}
.

If
\lnot\varphi\in\Gamma_{i}
, then consider the SC
FO(ID)
\xspace
\xspace
-derivation

(
\lnot
L)

\Gamma_{i},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\varphi,\Delta_{i}
\Gamma_{i},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{i}

\Gamma_{i},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\lnot\varphi,\Delta_{i}
is not cut-free provable, for otherwise,
\Gamma_{i},\Phi_{1},\dots,\Phi_{n}\vdash\Delta_{i}
